2437. THOMAS ADAMS was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of July, 1 watch, value 2 l.; 1 seal, 1 s.; and 1 watch-key, 6 d.; the goods of Henry Greenland.
HENRY GREENLAND . I live in Weymouth-street, New-road. On the 14th of July I was at work at Mr. Howe's, at Chiswick—I left at half-past one o'clock, and left my watch by accident in the water-closet—I was away till about five—I then missed my watch—I returned, and inquired of the prisoner and all the persons on the premises about it—the prisoner was working in the garden—I asked him if he had seen it—he said "No"—I am quite sure I told the prisoner I had lost it—this is my watch, seal, and key.
JOHN SIMPKIN . I am a pawnbroker. This watch was pawned at our shop by a man—this is the counterpart of the duplicate I gave him.
RICHARD HANCOCK ( police-sergeant T 10.) I took the prisoner—he said he knew nothing about it—I asked him his name—he said, "Adams"—I said, "It was pawned in the name of Adams"—I took him to the station, and in his pocket-book I found the duplicate of the watch, dated the 2nd of Aug.
Prisoner's Defence. The prosecutor came and said he had lost it; I said I knew nothing of it; he said, "Should you come in contact with it, you will know who it belongs to;" I found it afterwards as I was cleaning the sewer; I had to come to town with my master's horse; he gave me 5 s. 1 1/2 d. for that; I came away without any money of my own, and I was obliged to get some refreshment out of my master's money, and having the watch I pawned it to make up the deficiency.
GUILTY . Aged 37.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18430821-2438]
2438. JOHN DALEY was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of Aug., 1 bag, value 2 d.; and 28lbs. weight of shot, value 5 s.; the goods of Thomas Watson.
THOMAS WATSON . I keep an oil-shop, in Shadwell. I lost a bag containing 28lbs. of shot from my door on the 7th of Aug.—I found it in about five minutes at a door in Albert-street, which is near me—I do not know how it got there.
JAMES HATCH . I saw the prisoner take the bag from the prosecutor's door—he took it up Albert-street, and threw it at a door—it was too heavy for him to run with—I told the prosecutor, and he went after it.
Prisoner. I was coming home, and a gentleman collared me, and said I had stolen the shot.
GUILTY .** Aged 15.— Transported for Seven Years.—Ship.
[trialAccount t18430821-2439]
2439. JOHN NABBS was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of August, 100 cigars, value 14 s., the goods of Charles Butler and another; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
CHARLES BUTLER . I am in partnership with Charles Lambert. On the 7th of August I was at breakfast in my parlour—I heard a noise, and came down—I found these cigars had been removed from the case, and put on the counter—the prisoner was then in custody.
JOSEPH BROMLEY . I was sitting in my shop, next door to the prosecutor's,
about half-past eight o'clock, reading a book—I saw the prisoner walking backwards and forwards—I then missed him—I went to the prosecutor's door—I saw him go in, push by a board, shove the lid off the chest of cigars, and take these out—I stepped up and asked what he was going to do—he said, "Nothing"—I said, "What do you want here?"—he said, "I don't want anything"—I said again, "What do you want?"—he said, "I don't know"—I shut the door, and called my man to get the police.
Prisoner. on took them out of the chest yourself, and laid them on the counter, then said that I did it.
EDWIN ROBINS ( police-constable F 66.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from the Sessions-house, Clerkenwell—( read)—he is the person.
GUILTY . Aged 19.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18430821-2440]
2440. CHARLES SHIRLEY was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of August, 1 handkerchief, value 6 d., the goods of Edward Francis Rimbault, from his person; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
EDWARD FRANCIS RIMBAULT . I live in Denmark-street, St. Giles's. On the 6th of August, between eleven and twelve o'clock, I was coming through High-street, St. Giles's, with three ladies—the prisoner tried to snatch a scarf from a lady who was walking with me, but did not succeed—the policeman came up and said he would take him—I then went to the station, and there found my handkerchief—I had not missed it, but it is mine, and I had then lost it.
Prisoner. Q. Is there any mark on it? A. No; but I positively swear to it—I had put it into my pocket just before I came out, because it was worth nothing, having lost so many in the neighbourhood.
GEORGE JOHN RESTIEAUX ( policeman.) About twelve o'clock on Sunday night, the 6th of August, I saw the prisoner with three others—the prisoner left the others, and went behind the prosecutor—he tried to get the lady's scarf, and did not—he ran off very fast—I spoke to the prosecutor, and then went and took the prisoner, as I knew where to find him—I found on him this handkerchief.
Prisoner. I told him I bought it at Kennington; I told him the colour before he took it out of my pocket.
JOSEPH WHEELER . I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—the prisoner is the man.
GUILTY . Aged 19.— Transported for Ten Years.
[trialAccount t18430821-2441]
2441. WINIFRED BUTLER was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of August, 1 blanket, value 3 s.; 1 yard of ribbon, 2 d.; and 1 knife, 2 d.; the goods of Thomas Joyce, her master.
WINIFRED JOYCE . I am the wife of Thomas Joyce: we keep a lodging-house; the prisoner slept at my house occasionally for a night or two. On the 15th of August she was going away—I asked if the blankets were all right—she said they were—she then came to the shop and asked me for 1 s. or 1 s. 6 d.—I saw the blanket down below the skirt of her gown—I laid hold of it and said, "What is this?"—she said, "Nothing"—she tried to get away—I called my husband, and it turned out to be this blanket—she had no business with it—it is mine—this knife belongs to me—it was found in her pocket.
JEREMIAH CORNIN ( police-constable S 318.) I found this knife in the prisoner's pocket, and this blanket round her waist.
Prisoner's Defence. I got a severe fall down stairs, and was very much
hurt; I asked her for 1 s. 6 d. to go to the hospital; I said I would take this blanket with me; I intended to bring it back.
GUILTY . Aged 56.— Confined Three Months.

2442. ALFRED HUGHES and THOMAS BROWN were indicted for stealing, on the 18th of August, 1 handkerchief, value 6 d., the goods of Charles Walters, from his person.
CHARLES WALTERS . I am servant to Richard Williams. On the 18th of August, about eight o'clock, I was in Hyde-park—I received information, and missed my handkerchief—this is it—there is no mark on it, but I believe it is mine.
JONATHAN WITCHER ( police-sergeant A 27.) On the 18th of August, about eight o'clock in the morning, I was in Hyde-park, and saw the prisoners together—they went behind the prosecutor—Hughes put his hand to the prosecutor—I was not certain whether he took anything or not, but I spoke to the prosecutor, and he said he had lost a handkerchief—the prisoners were taken by another officer—I saw this handkerchief found on Brown.
Brown. I came out of the water, and found this handkerchief between the trees, between two gentlemen's feet; I picked it up, and two or three persons saw me.
HUGHES — GUILTY . Aged 20.
BROWN — GUILTY . Aged 12.
Cofined Three Months.

2444. FRANCIS SEYMOUR was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of July, 6 forks, value 2 l. 15 s., the goods of George Cowie, his master; and ELIZA SEYMOUR for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
JOHN POOLE . I am shopman to Mr. James Bassett, a pawnbroker, in Great Queen-street. On the 24th of July, the prisoner Eliza brought me six dessert-forks, and asked me to lend her as much as I could on them—having suspicion that they were not her husband's property, I gave them to my employer—I weighed them—these now produced correspond with them in weight, and pattern, and every thing.
Cross-examined by MR. CHARNOCK. Q. She is married to the male prisoner, is she not? A. Yes—these forks are not a common pattern—I should not hesitate to swear that these are the same.
GEORGE HAINS BREWER . I am in Mr. Bassett's service. I saw Eliza Seymour offer these forks—she said they were her husband's, who was a manufacturer of silver in a small way—my employer returned them, and desired me to follow her, which I did—she went down Drury-lane, and was met by Francis Seymour and another man—they went to the Old George—I told the policeman, and he took them—these forks were not found till the day after.
JAMES BROWN ( police-constable F 142.) I went to the public-house in Drury-lane, and saw Eliza Seymour come out—I followed her down Drury-lane, and stopped her—I asked her what she had done with the forks she had offered to pawn at Mr. Bassett's—she said she had given them to her husband—she took me back to the public-house where her husband had been, but he was gone—I took her to the station—she said her husband was employed at Mr. Cowie's, in Long-acre—I went there and took him—I said I
had his wife in custody for offering forks at Mr. Bassett's—he said he knew nothing about it—these forks were found the next day by another witness.
Cross-examined. Q. You found nothing on him? A. Only some silver.
ALFRED HARRISON . I am in the service of Mr. George Cowie. Francis Seymour was in his service—I remember the officer coming on the 25th of July—Gillmore brought these to the officer.
Cross-examined. Q. How long has this man been in your master's employ? A. About seven years—he is steady in business—this woman sometimes comes to bring his meals—these forks are not a common pattern—we do not make them—we may have sold three or four dozen of this pattern.
JAMES GILLMORE . I am in Mr. Cowie's employ. I found these forks behind a plate-chest in the pantry—the prisoner Francis had access to that pantry.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you believe these to be Mr. Cowie's? A. I cannot say—of course he had fiddle-pattern forks.
ALFRED HARRISON re-examined. On an examination of our stock and our books, I have no doubt these are our forks.
Cross-examined. Q. Do not you sometimes sell things which you do not enter? A. We have done so—these forks are about the common weight—these weigh exactly the same as those did that were taken to the pawnbroker's.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18430821-2445]
2445. FRANCIS SEYMOUR was again indicted for stealing, on the 15th of June, 4 sauce-ladles, value 2 l. 12 s., the goods of George Cowie, his master; and ELIZA SEYMOUR for feloniously receiving the same, wellknowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
JOHN POOLE . I am shopman to Mr. Bassett, of Great Queen-street. On the 15th of June, the prisoner Eliza pawned a pair of silver sauce-ladles for 15 s., and said they were her husband's.
RICHARD SAYER . I am in the service of James Sayer, a pawnbroker, in Drury-lane. On the 20th of July Eliza Seymour brought this pair of sauceladles to our house.
Cross-examined by MR. CHARNOCK. Q. These are a common pattern, are they not? A. I suppose so.
ALFRED HARRISON . I am in the service of Mr. George Cowie. I do not know these ladles—I do not know whether my master had such.
THOMAS HARRISON . I was in Mr. Cowie's employ. I have examined these ladles—they are part of his stock—the prisoner Francis had access to where these were.
Cross-examined. Q. Has Mr. Cowie any partner? A. No—these are a common pattern—I have left Mr. Cowie six years ago—I know these ladles were in his stock ten years ago.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18430821-2446]
2446. BENJAMIN HENMAN was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of June, 2 window-curtains and valances, value 1 l. 15 s.; and 13 rings, 5 s.; the goods of Richard William Pyne.
MR. CARTEEN conducted the Prosecution.
RICHARD WILLIAM PYNE . I keep the Bell in Wellington-street, Strand. In March last I had a furnished house in Charles-street, Covent-garden—I gave it up because the lease was expired—I had another place in White Horse-yard, Holborn, and removed my furniture there in the latter end of March—in the beginning of June I went to the place in White Horse-yard, and found a square of glass out, by which a person could open the door—I missed six
or seven beds, the curtain valances, brass rings, and many other things—I removed my things to Mr. Foster's, I think on the 15th of July—I attended a sale there, and saw the curtains which I had lost—I got a policeman, and, from some information, I went to the prisoner—he said he had bought the curtains of a man at Mr. Blackwell's public-house, in Holborn—I had not removed these curtains to Mr. Foster's—they were not to be sold for me, but for a Mr. Henman, the prisoner's brother, and were entered in his name—he said he worked for Mr. Walker, in Holborn, whose van stood in White Horse-yard—the prisoner had access to that yard at all times.
HORATIO HASLEHAM . I am an auctioneer, and live in Jerusalem-passage. Some time between the 1st and the 27th of June the prisoner came to me and brought these curtains to me for sale.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Have you known him some years? A. Yes, five or six—I put these curtains up twice without selling them—I had advanced 8 s. on them—the prisoner is in the upholstery line, in the employ of Mr. Walker, in Holborn—it is quite common for brokers to buy articles at one auctioneer's and to take them to another—it was the third time these curtains were put up that the prisoner's brother bought them—I knocked them down to him as an ordinary customer—I do not know whether he was aware that they were his brother's—the prisoner bad brought articles to me for sale before.
JOHN JAMES ALLEN ( police-constable E 159.) I took the prisoner—he said he bought the curtains at Mr. Blackwell's.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18430821-2447]
2447. JOHN JONES was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of Aug., 19 bolts, value 5 s.; 78 pairs of hinges, 10 s. 6 d.; 312 screws, 2 s.; 10 latches, 1 s. 9 d.; 2 sash-pullies, 10 d.; 3 window-fastenings, 10 d.; 4 sash-weights, 1 s. 2 d.; 12 yards of sash-line, 6 d.; and 20 lbs. weight of nails, 4 s.; the goods of Thomas Rook, his master.
THOMAS ROOK . The prisoner was in my service. I missed nineteen bolts, the hinges, and other things—these produced are mine—the prisoner said they belonged to me.
Cross-examined by MR. HDDDLESTON. Q. He said he took them to take care of? A. Yes—I have about fourteen workmen in my houses in Nelson-street, Hackney-road—it was locked up every night—there were no locks on the back doors, but bolts—I saw these articles about four days before—they were at the office—I had complained that there were some things lost from these houses—I had not told my workmen that they should be answerable for things that were lost—I considered the prisoner was liable for them, because he had the key every night—a person might get in if the windows were left open, but they were fastened every night, as the prisoner told me—there were bolts on all the doors, back and front, and on the window-shutters.
JAMES BYERLEY . I was at my stall on the 31st of July, between six and eight o'clock. The prisoner came and asked if I would buy some things—he said he had got several ironmongery things, and they were not right—(I thought he meant that they were stolen)—I asked where he lived—he told me, and I said I would go there that night or the next morning—I went the next day, and saw them—I went again with the policeman, and he took him and the things—he asked 10 s. 7 d. for them the first time I saw them, and said they were his own.
Cross-examined. Q. What are you? A. I deal in iron, and have done so for two or three years—I never bought things that were not right to my knowledge—my house was searched seven years ago, not for iron, but for bed-tick—I was
taken to Worship-street, and was there discharged, as there was no evidence—the prisoner came to me on Monday—I went to the policeman the same evening, and he told me to go and see if I could see any bills, or find who the property belonged to—I went to the prisoner's house on the Tuesday night.
HENRY CHARLES BARKER ( police-serjeant 11 H.) I went to the prisoner's house with Byerley—I said to the prisoner, "You have some ironmongery you have offered for sale to this man"—he said, "I have nothing here but what is my own"—Byerley pointed out these things—I found these in the bottom drawer, and four sash-weights in the bedroom—while I was searching the drawers I heard a scuffle and saw the prisoner halfway out of the street-door—the other officer had hold of him.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you know Byerley? A. I have known him nine years—I took him to Worship-street—he came to me about eleven at night, and I told him to go and look at the things—he shewed me a piece of paper with the prisoner's address—I did not go with him to the prisoner till the Friday night.
GUILTY . Aged 32.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18430821-2448]
2448. GEORGE FRY and MARIA LANGLEY were indicted for stealing, on the 15th of February, 1 table cloth, value 1 s. 3 d.; 1 flannel shirt, 1 s.; 1 candlestick, 1 s. 6 d.; 1 flat iron, 4 d.; and 1 blanket, 1 s. 6 d.; the goods of Jeremiah Lyons: and that Langley had been before convicted.
MARY LYONS . I am the wife of Jeremiah Lyons, and live in Long-lane, Ratcliffe. The prisoners both lodged at my house—when they went away I missed a blanket, a table-cloth, a flannel shirt, and a flat-iron—these are them.
Fry. I have a pension from the East India Company—it was due on the 22nd of August—there was 2 l. 12 s. due to me. Witness. He owes me 3 l. 12 s.—the key was sent home by another lodger, and I found all the things gone—there were not any things borrowed of me.
Langley. Every article here has been lent to us. Witness. No, they have not—I lent her the flat-iron to iron with, not to get a quartern of rum on—the flannel shirt was not lent to you to pledge with a piece of diaper.
GEORGE MURRAY ( police-constable 68 K.) I took the prisoners. The duplicate of the flat-iron was given me by Langley, and two duplicates of a table cloth and a flannel shirt were given me by Mrs. Lyons—Langley said what she had done was under the direction of Fry that Mrs. Lyons had given her permission to pledge these articles.
THOMAS ALLEN . I am a pawnbroker. I produce a table cloth and a flannel shirt, which were pledged by Langley in the name of "Fry, 7, High-street"—they correspond with the duplicate I have.
Langley. Q. Do you remember my separating the shirt from the diaper? A. No—but I recollect Mrs. Lyons saying, when she came to make inquiries, that there was a flat-iron, a shirt, and some other articles, but the table cloth she had lent to Langley—I did not tell the magistrate that the prosecutrix said that.
MARY LYONS re-examined. I did not tell Allen that I had lent the cloth to Langley—the policeman was with me.
GEORGE MURRAY re-examined. I went with Mrs. Lyons—I did not hear her tell Allen so.
Langley's Defence. The whole of the articles were lent to us some months ago when my husband was out of employ—the flannel shirt was first pawned with a piece of diaper, which I redeemed—she allowed me to pledge the bed
clothes from December till the week we were taken—the used to lend her lodgers articles to pledge, and then give them into custody.
CHARLES PALMER ( police-inspector.) I produce a certificate of Langley's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—the prisoner is the person.
LANGLEY — GUILTY . Aged 38.— Confined One Year.
FRY — NOT GUILTY .

2450. JOSEPH JOHNSON and GEORGE WATTS were indicted for stealing, on the 12th of August, 8 pairs of boots, value 1 l., the goods of Eliza Jay; and that Johnson had been before convicted of felony.
JOHN NEWTON . I live in Ward's-place, Islington. About half-past nine o'clock, on Saturday night, the 12th of August, I saw Johnson cut a bundle of Adelaide boots from Mrs. Jay's door, and give them to Watts—he ran away with them—I am sure these are the two men—I informed the police.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. You knew Watts by his flannel jacket, did not you? A. Yes, and by his face—I knew him before—he was standing at the top of Ward's-place—I was in the road, just opposite him, walking up and down the Lower-road—the boots were found in Ward's-place—I did not see them picked up.
HENRY PARKER . I live in Ward's-place. On Saturday night I saw Watts run down the place, with a good many pairs of boots under his arm—I found one pair near to where he ran—I gave them to my mother.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you see Newton? A. No—there were several persons running down Ward's-place—I know Newton very well—the prisoners did not live in Ward's-place—I swear that Watts was the person.
ANN PARKER . My son Henry gave me this pair of boots that night—I took them back to Mrs. Jay's.
ELIZA JAY . bout half-past nine o'clock I heard the string of the bundle of boots cut—I went to the door, and missed them—I believe this to be one of the pairs.
THOMAS TYLER ( police-constable N 275.) I produce a certificate of Johnson's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—the prisoner is the person.
JOHNSON — GUILTY . Aged 20.— Transported for Ten Years.
WATTS †— GUILTY . Aged 20.— Transported for Seven Years.

2455. GEORGE CARR was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of August, I pint of ale, value 5 d., the goods of Thomas Pickford and others, his masters.
Mr. Prendergast conducted the Prosecution. George Lightfoot. About nine o'clock at night, on Saturday, the 12th of August, I was employed as wagoner, at Mr. Pickford's wharf, in the City-road—I was near the bank where the goods are landed—I saw the prisoner and another near the bank—I afterwards spoke to Hawkins, the watchman—the prisoner was standing at the end of the bank, and when I came back with my ropes he was gone—he appeared to be watching Hawkins—the ale was deposited under a shed, within three or four yards of the bank—as I was going past I heard the pail handle rattle—I informed Hawkins—I had then missed the prisoner and the other man from where they had been standing—the shed is rather dark—Hawkins went away from me directly.
Cross-examined by MR. WILDE. Q. How long have you been in the prosecutor's employ? A. Nearly twenty-nine years—the prisoner has been with them three or four years.
CALEB HAWKINS . I am a watchman in the employ of Messrs. Pickfords. At half-past nine o'clock at night, on the 12th of August, I was on duty on the bank, and saw the prisoner—I had received particular orders to watch some casks of ale there, in consequence of some pilferings—I received information from Lightfoot—I afterwards went towards the shed where the ale had been deposited—I heard the pail, and the liquor running from the cask—I went in directly, and there found the prisoner, in a bending attitude—the pail was against the head of the cask, and the liquor running into it—I laid hold of him—I found the pail contained ale—I took it to the lodge-keeper—the prisoner said, "For God's sake, have mercy on me; consider my wife and three children."
Cross-examined. Q. How long have you been in the employ? A. Twentytwo years—this was Burton ale—I am sixty-nine years old—I use spectacles, but not to look at a man in the face—I knew the prisoner well.
MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Did you look at the cask afterwards? A. Yes—I got a lantern, and found a hole made with a gimlet, and a peg in it—that is the hole where the liquor came from—I said to the prisoner, "Carr, it is no use, I must have the pail."
WILLIAM WHITE ( police-constable G 105.) The prisoner was given into custody for stealing the ale—he said he hoped I should have mercy on him, for his wife and family; he was sorry for what he had done.
JAMES WARD . I am a clerk in the employ of Messrs. Pickfords. These goods are in the custody of Thomas Pickford and others.
GUILTY . Aged 35.— Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18430821-2456]
2456. JOHN MASON was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of August, 1 handkerchief, value 1 s., the goods of Theophilus Caudlot, from his person.
THEOPHILUS CAUDLOT . I am a cook, and live in Chapel-street West, May-fair. About a quarter past ten o'clock in the evening of the 15th of August I was in Regent-street—I had information, and missed my handkerchief from my hind coat-pocket—I knew it was there a few minutes before—a person pointed out the prisoner, who was on the other side of the street—I went over, and asked if he had not got a handkerchief that did not belong to him, and, after some time, he dropped it down, and said it was not him—Skinner picked it up, and gave it me—this is it, and it is marked with my name.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. You have no doubt about it? A. No.
JOHN GRAY ( police-sergeant C 14.) I received charge of the prisoner in Regent-street—he said it was quite a mistake—I took him to the station.
SARAH SKINNER . I was walking with my sister, in Regent-street—I saw the prisoner put his hand into the gentleman's pocket, take a silk handkerchief, and put it into his own pocket—he then dropped it—I picked it up, and gave it to the prosecutor.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 22.— Confined Four Months.

2457. WILLIAM HENRY JACKSON and FREDERICK BARTLETT were indicted for stealing, on the 14th of Aug., 1 handkerchief, value 4 s., the goods of George Dent, from his person.
GEORGE DENT . I live in Globe-terrace, Lower-road, Islington. On the afternoon of the 14th of Aug., I was in Britannia-fields—there was a meeting there of Father Mathew's—I felt something—I turned, and saw Bartlett handing my handkerchief to Jackson, who had his hand under his blouse, ready to receive it—I took it from him—I gave Jackson into custody—Bartlett was brought up by a gentleman, and I recognized him immediately.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Was any one with you? A. yes—I am sure the handkerchief had got into Jackson's hand.
JAMES ARDIN . I was with the prosecutor, and saw the prisoner—the prosecutor took the handkerchief from Jackson.
WILLIAM LEATHER ( police-constable N 168.) I received Jackson in charge, and Bartlett was then brought up.
Bartlett's Defence. I never saw the handkerchief till I saw it at the office—I saw a mob, and went up, and the gentleman took me.
(The prisoner received a good character.)

2461. JAMES PAUL was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of August, 30lb. weight of copper, value 1 l., the goods of John Henry Vivian, and others, in a certain vessel, in a certain port of entry and discharge.—2nd Count, stating it to be the goods of Charles Grange Weller.
WILLIAM CAMERON . I am boatswain of the ship Madagascar —she was taking in her cargo in the West India Docks—part of it was copper. On the 19th of Aug., I was in the forecastle—I looked aft, and saw the prisoner standing in the gangway—he had two tyes of copper—I went to him and asked what he had to do with those two tyes of copper, and where he got them—he said from one of the lumpers who were working in the hold—I detained him, and the commanding officer came on board, and went for a policeman—these are the tyes of copper.
SAMUEL AVERY . I am constable of the Docks. I took the prisoner, who was in the ship in the Docks—I found one of these plates of copper between his skin and the waistband of his trowsers—the other he had slid down by his side, because he said it hurt him—I asked how he came by them—he said one of the lumpers, a tall Irishman, with grey whiskers, promised to give him 6 d. if he would take them on shore, and leave them there till night—I took him to the men, and then he said it was not either of them, and one of the men said, "You scoundrel, it was only two days ago you stole the watch."
JAMES RUDGE . I am first officer of the Madagascar —he commander's name is Charles Grange Weller, and the shipper's is John Henry Vivian.
Prisoner's Defence. A man said, "If you take it on shore, lay it against the wall, I will give you 4 d.;" he took the handkerchief off my neck, and tied it round me.
GUILTY .* Aged 14.— Transported for Ten Years.
[trialAccount t18430821-2462]
2462. THOMAS SMITH and PATRICK BARRETT were indicted for stealing, on the 5th of August, 5 penknives, value 5 s., the goods of Samuel Weller and another.
SAMUEL WELLER . I am a broker, and live in Russell-court, Drury-lane; I have one partner. About half-past ten o'clock on Saturday night, the 5th of August, I was in my parlour—I saw the two prisoners come into the shop—Smith came in first; Barrett stood behind him at the door—Barrett took something off the counter, which was just at the door—I could not see what it was—I came out, and he ran off—I called, "Stop him"—I told my lad to keep Smith while I went after Barrett, but he was gone—when I came back I missed these double-bladed pen-knives, which had been in front of the counter—I asked Smith if he knew the other person—he said he knew nothing at all about him—I gave Smith into custody—the knives have not been found.
WILLIAM MILLER . I am in the prosecutor's employ. At about a quarter-past ten o'clock on the night of the 5th of August, I saw the two prisoners at the tobacconist's at the corner of Russell-court—they were in conversation together—that was three or four minutes before they came to our shop—when
I came back Smith was in custody—I am quite sure he is the one whom I had wen with Barrett before.
WILLIAM BAFFIN ( police-constable F 141.) I was called to the prosecutor's shop, and received charge of Smith—he said he came to look at a coat, and ask the price, and during that time the other committed the robbery.
WILLIAM WEST ( police constable F 106.) On the morning of the 7th of August, I went to Lincoln's-court, Drury-lane, and found Barrett in bed—I knocked—his mother opened the door, and asked what I wanted—I said her son—she said, "What has he done?"—Barrett jumped up in bed and said, "It is nothing, only for being with Tom Monton on Saturday night."
Smith's Defence. We were together in Clare-market; I left him at half-past nine o'clock; I went to Russell-court; I was taken into custody because he said a boy behind me had done something.
Barrett's Defence. I never was with him there; I did not know where he went to.
SMITH** — GUILTY . Aged 18.
BARRETT** — GUILTY . Aged 17.
Confined Twelve Months.
[trialAccount t18430821-2463]
2463. LAVINIA ROBERTS, MARGARET PATTERSON , and MARY TANSLEY , were indicted for stealing, on the 9th of August, 1 saw, value 2 s.; the goods of John Thurlow; and 1 walking-stick, value 9 d., the goods of John Wood.
RICHARD DONOVAN . I live in Harris-court, Shadwell. On the 9th of August I was at work in an empty house in Phbe-street, Limehouse—I and my mate went to the Steam Packet public-house, about twelve o'clock, to get some dinner—the three prisoners were in the tap-room—they asked my mate to stand a pot of beer, and he would nob—we went to our work at one o'clock—the prisoners followed us, and all came on the premises where we were at work—they staid about a quarter of an hour, and went away together—when the carpenter came he missed the saw, and a man lost his stick.
Patterson. Q. Did not you tell us to follow, you? A. No—I did not see Roberts kiss the old man.
JOHN THURLOW . I am a carpenter, and was at work at the empty house in Phbe-street. I left, to go to dinner, with Donovan—I went back, and missed a saw, which I had left in the back parlour—I spoke to a policeman, and went with him to the White Horse—the officer went in, and Patterson came out—I gave her in charge—I had seen her in Poplar—the other two prisoners then came out—I asked if they had got my saw—they all denied it—before we got to the station, Roberts said, "I will tell you the truth, I sold, the saw for 6 d.; here is 2 d., and 4 d. we spent for a pot of beer"—I did not wish to press the charge, but the policeman said we must go to the station—this is my saw.
JANE CLAYTON . I am the wife of William Clayton, a broker. On the 9th of August Roberts and Tansley came to our shop—Tansley staid outside—Roberts came in and asked me if I would buy this saw—I said I was not much of a judge of it—I gave her 6 d. for it.
THOMAS BARTLETT ( police-sergeant K 16.) I took the prisoners—they said they knew nothing about it—Roberts then said she received the saw of Tansley, and sold it for 6 d., and she showed me where it was.
Roberts's Defence. I did not know there was any thing gone, till the saw was given into my hand to sell.
Patterson's Defence. I never touched the article; I was in company with those who did touch it; neither I nor Tansley went in to sell it.
Tansley's Defence. I received the saw from Patterson and delivered it to Roberts.
ROBERTS — GUILTY . Aged 26.
PATTERSON — GUILTY . Aged 25.
TANSLEY — GUILTY . Aged 27.
Recommended to Mercy.— Confined
Seven Days.
[trialAccount t18430821-2464]
2464. ELIZABETH GEORGE was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of August, 2 pair of stays, value 13 s., the goods of Charles Longland.
EMILY BIRD . I live in the service of Mr. Charles Longland, a haber-dasher, in Britannia-row, Hoxton. On the evening of the 9th of August I was behind the counter—I saw the prisoner and two other girls looking at some handkerchiefs at the door—there were some stays there—two of the girls said, "Come along"—they all went away together—after they were gone I missed two pair of stays from the door—they were Mr. Longland's.
WILLIAM WILLIAMS . I am going on for thirteen years of age, and live with my father in Cross-street, St. Luke's—at eight o'clock in the evening, on the 9th of August, I was in Myrtle-street, Hoxton, which is near Mr. Longland's shop—I saw the prisoner and two little girls running as fast as they could—I saw the prisoner had a pair of stays partly under her shawl—one of the girls said, "What a lark this is."
WALTER HENRY BROWN ( police-constable G 62.) I took the prisoner on the 10th of August—she denied the charge.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18430821-2465]
2465. FRANCIS SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of August, 49 yards of printed cotton, value 2 l.; and 45 yards of mouselin de laine, 1 l., the goods of Richard Gunton.
THOMAS PLUMMER . I am shopman to Richard Gunton, linen-draper, in Lamb's Conduit-street—this cotton-print and mouselin de laine is my master's—on Tuesday evening, the 15th of August, a person spoke to me—I went to the door and missed these articles—they had been safe inside the door about an hour before.
JOHN WATSON . I live in Harper's-mews, Theobald's-road—on Tuesday evening, the 15th of August, I was opposite Mr. Gunton's shop—I saw the prisoner and another person walking backwards and forwards—the prisoner then stood at the corner of the shop—we went over to Lamp-office-court and saw the prisoner take the bundle of things from just inside the shop door, and give them to the other one, who was at the corner of Chapel-street—he went up Chapel-street in the dark, and the prisoner after him—we followed them round Bedford-row to Red Lion-square and told the policeman—the other one then chucked down the bundle and ran away—the prisoner did not run—the policeman took him—he said, "I cannot walk along the street without these young vagabonds giving me in charge"—I knew him before.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. You have been in Horsemonger-lane, have you not? A. Never in my life, nor in any other jail—I left my place last week, and work with my father at Mr. Bosher's, a green-grocer—I know Johnson—it was not a very dark night—I had been bathing in Chalk-road, Maiden-lane—I did not see Johnson that night—I have known Brown six years—he and I had been together from ten in the morning—I went with him to sell his apples, and when he was done I went home—then I went out, and Brown and I went to the Chalk-road—he lives in Eagle-court, not far from me—it was not a rainy night—it was about a quarter past eight o'clock—there was a sailor boy with the prisoner—the sailor was in custody at Clerkenwell—I stated there what I have here, but the sailor was discharged—the magistrate did not say he could not rely on my evidence, but I could not swear to him because he had different clothes on—he was the person whom I
supposed I had seen run away—I did not point him out—a person named Dollin pointed him out—Brown could not swear to him—the prisoner was not in custody when the sailor was discharged—I did not see any lads playing about the prosecutor's shop—there were two or three ladies walked past—I have known the sailor boy by sight—I have not had any communication with his friends, or asked one of them for money not to come up against him—I have not spoken about having 3 s. 6 d. a day for coming up here—the prisoner's mother offered me 5 s. to go into the country—I did not speak to her first about it—we were coming up Holborn, and she met me and said, "If you like to take 5 s. a piece I will give it you, and on Wednesday I will give you 1 l."—Johnson was in my company when I saw the prisoner take these things—he was not before the magistrate—I did not say that he was—he was there ready to speak against the prisoner—I did not say to Johnson when I saw the prisoner, "There is a couple of prigs, let us watch them,"nor any thing of the kind, nor say Johnson was a fool to talk about not knowing the parties who took it, nor any thing to that effect—Johnson did not say it was impossible to recognize the parties—neither I nor Brown said, "Come, let us swear that Johnson did not say 'We cannot do that, as we did not see him take it'"—I did not say "Never mind that, you fool; if we do not we shall get nothing"—nothing of that sort passed.
COURT. Q. You said you could not swear to the sailor boy, but you could to the prisoner? A. Yes—I knew the prisoner before—I had seen him tossing.
WILLIAM BROWN . On Tuesday evening, the 15th of Aug., I was with Watson in Lamp-office-court—we saw the prisoner and another walking past Mr. Gunton's shop—the prisoner went and took the pieces, and gave them to the other who went up Chapel-street—the prisoner stood at the corner for two or three minutes, and then he went after him—they went round Bedford-row, and Princes-street to Red Lion-square—I saw the policeman and gave the prisoner into custody—the other one threw the bundle down and ran away.
Cross-examined. Q. What are you? A. A plasterer's boy, but I have no work—I have been selling fruit in the street—I was not in custody for stealing fat—we saw the prisoner about half-past eight o'clock—it was dark—I and Watson had been bathing—Johnson was with us—Watson said, "There are two thieves, let us watch them"—he did not say we should get 3 s. 6 d. a day—we were standing together, and Johnson behind us in the court—he could see just as far as we could—I was before the Magistrate when the sailor boy was there—I could not identify him, and he was discharged—Johnson did not say in my hearing that he could not swear to the prisoner being the one who took the bundle—he would not swear anything of the kind—the prisoner and the other man had gone about a quarter of a mile from the prosecutor's shop, when I saw the bundle thrown down—they walked fast, and we walked fast behind them—we did not meet a policeman all that time, but no one came between us and the persons we followed.
JOHN NORTH ( police-constable E 90.) On the evening of the 15th of Aug., these two witnesses pointed out the prisoner to me, and there was another man running away—Brown picked up the bundle, and the prosecutor identified the things—I told the prisoner he must go with me—he refused—I took him to the station.
JURY to JOHN WATSON. Q. Did you lose sight of the prisoner, from the time you first saw him till the other dropped the bundle? A. No.
MR. HORRY called
RICHARD JOHNSON . I am a wheelwright, and live in French-horn-yard,
Holborn, with my father. I was with Watson and Brown, in Lamb's Conduit-street, about eight o'clock, on the 15th of Aug.—Watson said, "There are two thieves, let us watch them, we shall get 3 s. 6 d. a day up at Newgate"—Brown and Watson then went a little way into Chapel-street—I was a little way before them—I was not in Lamp-office-court—it was rather dark—I did not see the person at Mr. Gunton's door—I saw the face of the other man, but not of the prisoner—I saw two persons in Chapel-street—their backs were towards me—the prisoner is not the person who had the bundle—he was taller than the prisoner—we followed the person who ran with the bundle—he was not joined by any one—we were following close behind him—I saw the bundle thrown down by the taller one, who then ran away—the prisoner was about three yards from the one who had the bundle—we called to the policeman to take the prisoner—I cannot say whether the prisoner was in company with the other, but he went the same road—I could not distinguish the countenance of the person who was with the one who had the bundle, and I had as good an opportunity of seeing him as Brown and Watson—when we were at the station I said, "We never saw the prisoner take it"—Watson said, "I see you will spoil it, we must swear it"—he said, "Never mind that, you fool, if we don't we shall get nothing"—I was at the Police-court, but I was not examined—nothing has been paid to me to come here to give evidence—I offered myself to the prisoner's mother.
COURT. Q. Where is Lamp-office-court? A. Just by Little Ormond-yard—it is near Chapel-street—the prosecutor's shop is at the corner of Chapel-street—I did not see any body take any thing from the shop—I was there watching about five minutes—I saw one person with a bundle—I did not see any other person join him, but we followed two persons—one was standing up Chapel-street, and one who I think was the prisoner was at the posts—I was not sure it was him when he was taken—he was taken in Red Lion-square—he was sent to Newgate—I did not go and tell his mother that I was there, and I did not know it was him—I did not know his mother then, but a young lad who worked at Parker's knew the prisoner's mother, and he came to my house—some of the boys told him that I was there—I told the prisoner's mother about the 3 s. 6 d. that they said they would get by swearing against the prisoner—it was Watson who spoke first about it.
ELIZA ANN HANNAGAN . I am the prisoner's mother—I know the witnesses—I met them together—they said if they had 5 s. a piece they could go into the country—my son lived at home with me.
COURT. Q. How does he get his living? A. He was out of a situation—he is generally at the bar of a public-house.
GUILTY .** Aged 17.— Transported for Seven Years.
NEW COURT.— Monday, August 28 th, 1843.
Fifth Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.

2471. MATILDA FOSTER was again indicted for stealing, on the 14th of June, 57lbs. weight of feathers, value 5 l. 14 s.; 2 pillows, 8 s.; I bolster, 15 s.; 3 blankets, 6 s.; 1 shirt, 2 s.; 2 pillow-cases, 1 s.; 1 flat-iron, 6 d.; gowns, 2 l.; 1 yard of velvet, 1 l.; 7 frocks, 2 l.; 1 veil, 1 l.; 2 petticoats, 5 l.; 2 shifts, 2 s.; 3 handkerchiefs, 1 s.; 1 bonnet, 1 l.; and 1 pair of snuffers, 1 s.; the goods of Isaac Wilbraham.
MARY WILBRAHAM . I am the wife of Isaac Wilbraham, and live in Aske-street, Hoxton. The prisoner took a furnished room of me—part of these things were in the room and part on the landing—I missed the whole of them—I distinctly swear to some of them.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Have you not sworn to all of them? A. No—I have no marks on them—I have had them many years—I can swear positively to this flannel petticoat of my child's and this quarter of a yard of silk velvet—this bed tick is mine to the best of my belief.
GEORGE HANSON . I am a pawnbroker. This velvet and petticoat were pawned for 3 s.—I cannot say who by—I have a blanket pawned in the name of Ann Foster—this is the counterpart of the duplicate—I cannot say whether the person who pawned that pawned the other things.
Cross-examined. Q. Were these pillows pawned at your shop? A. Yes, one of them, by a person named Pitcham—I do not know who pawned the other pillow, but I have every reason to believe it is not the prosecutor's.
HENRY LAMBERT ( police-sergeant M 2.) The duplicate of the blanket was found on the prisoner by a female—she said she had taken the blanket and the bolster—she bad stolen the blanket that this ticket relates to from, Mr. Wilbraham.
Cross-examined. Q. What was said to her? A. Mr. Wilbraham came to the station, and charged her in my presence with stealing a quantity of feathers,
blankets, and other articles—she said she had not taken the feathers, but she had taken the blankets and the bolster.
WILLIAM WALLIS ASHFORD . I produce these four pillows, two blankets, three frocks, and a gown, which I took in pledge from the prisoner.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY . Aged 26.— Confined Six Months.

2474. WILLIAM MATTHEWS was indicted for embezzlement.
MR. DOWLING conducted the Prosecution.
DANIEL HAYBITTLE . I am master of the work-house of the Strand Union—the prisoner was a pauper there—on the 24th of June I sent him to collect some money which was due from Mr. Toplis, he did not bring me that account—on the 8th of July, there was some money due from Mr. Pritchard and Mr. Homeyard—he did not account to me for either of them—I did not see him after the 8th of July, till he was in custody—we act under the board of guardians of the Strand Union, and the money belonged to them.
SAMUEL TOPLIS . I am a plasterer, and live in Cirencester-place, Marylebone—I paid the prisoner 1 l. 6 s. 8 d., on the 24th of June, for the guardians of the poor of the Strand Union.
JOHN PRITCHARD . I live in Cleveland-street, Fitzroy-square—on Saturday, the 8th of July, I paid the prisoner 10 s. 6 d. on account of the guardians of the poor of the Strand Union.
JAMES HOLMYARD . I am a plasterer, and live in Ogle-street—I paid the prisoner 1 l. 2 s., on the 8th of July, on account of the guardians of the poor.
Prisoner. I was an unpaid servant, and had been working there a long time.
GUILTY . Aged 33.— Confined Six Months.

2475. JOHN LEWIS was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of July, 26 numbers of Lloyd's Penny Atlas, value 1 s. 6 d.; the goods of Edward Lloyd, his master.
MR. PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
RICHARD WYNNE . I deal in cheap publications, and lire in Chichester-place, King's cross—on Friday evening, the 28th of July, the prisoner brought these twenty-six copies of Lloyd's Atlas—he asked if I wanted to buy any—I asked what he wanted for them—he said, "What would you give?"—I offered him 6 d.—he would not take that—I gave him 8 d.—I took them to Mr. Lloyd's next rooming, and marked them, so that I know them again—the prisoner came again to me, and I gave him into custody.
EDWARD LLOYD . I am printer and publisher of Lloyd's Penny Atlas—this is my property—on Friday, the 28th of July, none of these numbers had been published—they are worth 1 s. 6 d.—the prisoner applied for employment, and was in the place where he could take these.
Cross-examined by MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. Have you any partners? A. No—the prisoner has borne a good character.

2476. DANIEL SINKFIELD and WILLIAM STOKES were indicted for stealing, on the 13th of August, 77lbs. weight of coal, value 8 d.; the goods of Daniel Sturge; in a certain barge, in a certain navigable canal called Regent's canal.
JOHN DUNBAR . I am in the employ of Daniel Sturge—he has a barge called the Emma —on Saturday night, the 13th of August, she had 42 tons of coal in her, and was lying at Rosewell wharf, Regent's canal—I saw her next morning, and there was a small deficiency—the coals produced by the officer appear to be of the same quality.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Were all the coals found of the same quality? A. The officer produced 3 cwt.—some of them were different—such coals had been sold about at the chandler's shops—I think these are park of what were in the barge.
JOHN WALSH ( police-constable K 145.) I saw the prisoners coming from the Regent's canal bridge, in the morning of the 13th of August, where the barge Emma was, with two bags of coals on their backs—before I came down, Sinkfield was gone to his cottage, and the other had got a bag on his back—I am sure they both had coals—I showed these coals to Dunbar, and they corresponded with what were in the barge—this was the only barge laden with coals in the canal.
Cross-examined. Q. Where did you take them from? A. From the bag on Stokes back—I went into Sinkfield's cottage—I was shown some coals there, but I did not take any.
(The prisoners received good characters.)
SINKFIELD — GUILTY . Aged 30.
STOKES — GUILTY . Aged 46.
Confined Three Months.

[trialAccount t18430821-2478]
2478. ROBERT COUSINS was indicted for embezzlement.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
DANIEL WALTERS . I am a silk manufacturer, and live in Wilson-street, Finsbury. The prisoner was my clerk and town-traveller for seventeen or eighteen years, at a salary of 200 l. a-year, which was paid whenever he applied for it—it was his duty to receive money from my various customers, and to enter the sums on the day he received them in this day-book—if he received checks he was to pay them into my bankers, Smith, Payne, and Co., or to bring them home—I have been in the habit of leaving town for the country every day at four o'clock—I had some conversation with Mr. Costa's clerk, and, on the afternoon of the 5th of August, I called the prisoner's attention to Costa's account, which appears to be 33 l. 2 s.—it is in the prisoner's hand-writing—there appears no entry from Morrisson on the 3rd of July, of 5 l. 7 s., nor till the 15th of July, when there is an entry of 5 l.—there is no entry of 5 l. 11 s. 6 d. on the 23rd of June, from Mr. Watson.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. You and the prisoner are cousins by marriage? A. He married a cousin of my first wife's—I find in this book a check of 44 l. accounted for as paid into Smith's—the check was crossed, and must have found its way there—here is an entry of 5 l. 7 s., and of 5 l. 11 s.
MR. DOANE. Q. these appear entered on the 15th of July? A. Yes, they make up the 44 l.
JOHN WATSON . I am a draper, and live on Holborn-hill. On the 23rd of June I paid the prisoner 5 l. 11 s. 6 d. by a check on account of his master.
RICHARD WADE . I am clerk to Morrisson and Co., in Fore-street. On the 8th of July I paid the prisoner 5 l. 7 s.—the check was dated the 7th—I did not pay him any on the 15th of July.
JOHN GREY . I am clerk to Costa and Company. I paid the prisoner, on the 15th of July, a check for 44 l.
STEPHEN WALTERS . I am a solicitor, and the prosecutor's nephew. My attention was called to this by him on Monday, the 7th of August, I went to the warehouse, and saw the prisoner—when I entered he was in the room behind the counting-house—he came out, and I called his attention to the entry 33 l. 2 s.—he said he had received the 44 l., that it was a very dry argument, and the fact was he wanted the money then—I asked why he had not entered Morrisson's check—he said it appeared he had received it on the 30th—I said, "I don't see how it appears so"—I then said, "Why did you not enter it when you received it?"—he said he wanted the money—I then asked him about other transactions.
GUILTY . Aged 62.— Transported for Seven Years.
(There were two other indictments against the prisoner, and the prosecutor stated his loss to be 450 l.)

2482. WILLIAM BENNION was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of Aug., 1 parasol, value 1 s.; the goods of James Richardson Cody.

I was in a field at Paddington, at Father Mathew's teetotal meeting—this parasol was in my boy's hand—he slipped close to a mob, and dropped it, and it was gone in a moment—I do not know who took it—this is it, but it has been altered since I lost it—I know it by the handle—the top has been cut off and made shorter, and the hook has been altered—I took it to pieces myself once, to repair it—I believe it to be mine.

2483. GEORGE WILSON was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of August, 1 handkerchief, value 2 s. 6 d.; the goods of John Jenkins, from his person.
JOHN JENKINS . I live in St. John-street-road. On the 11th of August; about nine o'clock in the evening, I was at the corner of Wilderness-row, taking some oysters—I felt a pull at my pocket—I turned and saw the prisoner, and my handkerchief was in his hand, and partly out of my pocket. I collared him, and then my handkerchief came quite out and fell down—I am sure no one could have taken it out but him.
STEPHEN SADLER . I am a parish officer. I saw the prisoner attempting to get away, and took him to the station.
Prisoner. I was at the corner of Wilderness-row—the prosecutor turned and asked me for his handkerchief—I said I had not got it—he looked down and found it at his feet—there were several other persons besides me.
Prosecutor. There was no one near roe but him.
GUILTY .** Aged 20.— Transported for Ten Years.

2485. THOMAS MASON was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of August, 2 sixpences; the monies of Matthias Graves.
MATTHIAS GRAVES . I live in Bishop's-court, Clerkenwell. The prisoner? was lodging with me—I marked some shillings and two sixpences—when I was in bed on the 12th of August I heard a chink—next morning I called in the policeman, who found these two sixpences on the prisoner, which I had marked with a centre-bit—they had been in my left hand waistcoat pocket.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. When you heard the noise did you affect to be asleep? A. No, I was talking to the prisoner at the same time—he was talking about his honesty, and while he was pretending to pull off his clothes he robbed me—he said he had no money of his own—my clothes were on the box—he hunghisclothes on the door—there was about two feet between his clothes and mine—we had slept in the same room for seven or eight days—he owes me 1 l. 8 s. 4 1/2 d. for rent and money lent—I said I did not want to prosecute, all I wanted was my money, and I would recommend him to mercy if the money was paid—I heard the chink—he came to bed, and I went to sleep—next morning I got a policeman—I had turned waistcoat inside out, and laid it on the box, and when I came to it in the morning it was all in a rumple.
THOMAS CLARK ( police-sergeant 13 G.) I took the prisoner. I took up a waistcoat and said, "Is this your's?"—he said, "Yes"—I felt in the pocket of it, and found these two sixpences—he said it was his first offence, and he hoped the prosecutor would forgive him.
Cross-examined. Q. Did he not say he found them on the floor and put them into his pocket? A. Not when I took him—he said so at the magistrate's office.
COURT. Q. Did the prosecutor describe the marks on the sixpences before you found them? A. Yes, he shewed me a shilling, which had the same mark on them as the sixpences, and here is the punch which he said it marked them with.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 22.— Confined Three Months
[trialAccount t18430821-2486]

2488. EMMA MARIA CARTWRIGHT was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of Aug., 14 sixpences; the monies of Susan Richardson, her mistress.

I sent her with fourteen sixpences to my sister to pay the workwomen—she did not go with them—I met her afterwards in Charies-street—she said she had lost the money, and then said she had spent it—she had a new pair of boots and stockings on, which she said she bought with the money—she was always a good girl before.

[trialAccount t18430821-2489]
2489. SARAH LLOYD and RICHARD SEAGER were indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of Aug., 1 purse, value 1 d.; 1 sovereign; and 8 half-sovereigns; the property of Michael Buckley from his person.
MICHAEL BUCKLEY . Between seven and eight o'clock in the morning, on the 2nd of Aug., I was in Plumtree-street, St. Giles's, in the act of taking some silver out of my waistcoat pocket, as there were a few holes in it, I was putting the silver into my purse, when Lloyd came down bv my side, and snatched the purse out of my hand—it contained eight half sovereigns and one sovereign, which I had been hoarding up for some time—she ran down the street—I was running after her for about seven yards, when Seager came and threw me down.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Were you sober? A. I was perfectly sober enough to identify either of these—I had been out all night, walking about—I had had a pint of beer, and had been in a coffee-shop—they did not lock me up for being drunk, but for protection more than any thing else—I went after the policeman—there was a mob abont me—the Magistrate did not object to my being examined because I was not sober—I had seen Seager before Lloyd ran away with my purse—they were both in Plumtree-street—Seager was on the other side of the street, and Lloyd came across to me—I will not swear that Seager did not come out of a public-house when he heard a noise at the door—when he knocked me down I ex-tricated myself as well as I could, and weat away—we found him afterwards in George-street.
EDMUND NASH ( police-constable E 70.) Between seven and eight o'clock, that morning I saw a mob in Compton-street, The prosecutor told me he had been robbed of eight half-sovereigns and a sovereign—I went and stood at the corner of George-street for about two minutes, when a woman told me something—I ran up George-street, and saw a mob at the corner of Phnix-street—I asked the prosecutor if he could point out the woman and he
pointed out Lloyd—Seager then came over, and said, "What is the matter?"—the prosecutor said, "That is the man that knocked me down"—I took them both to the station.
Cross-examined. Q. The prosecutor had been drinking, had he not? A. Yes; and we locked him up for protection.
EDWARD HARRIS ( police-constable E 50.) I saw the prisoners standing near the watering place in Phnix-street. After they were taken, I searched the place where they had been standing, and found five half-sovereigns—I went to the station, came out, and made further search, and found this purse thrown over into the ruins in Plum tree-street, just opposite where I found the half-sovereigns—both the prisoners had been near that spot.
MR. PAYNE called
JOHN O'BRIEN . I keep a beer-house at Nos. 7 and 8, George-Street, Bloomsbury. I remember the morning when the prisoners were taken—Seager came to my place between seven and eight o'clock, and staid for half an hour—he asked me for a pint of the best ale, but being a little in liquor I would not serve him—I said the best plan would be to go home—a few minutes after be went out, as there was a disturbance at the end of the stone-yard.
COURT. Q. Do you know anything of the other witnesses who are coming? A. I know some of them, not all—several of them frequent my house—Stewart does occasionally—Tool lives next door to me—he was in my house at that time—Seager has been long in the habit of coming in occasionally—he is a tailor, and lives in Church-street, Soho—I always shut up at twelve o'clock at night, and open at eight, or sometimes at seven, in the morning—I always shut up the bar at twelve o'clock—a few single young men lodge with me—sometimes I have twenty; sometimes more or less—I have two houses, with a communication between them—when I shut the bar the lodgers come in at the private door—they go out sometimes at five or six o'clock in the mom ing—I am up till about two—if they do not come in by that hour I shut them out—they have no key—I keep the key.
JAMES STEWART . I am a hair-dresser, and live at No. 1, George-street, Bloomsbury. On the 2nd of August, between seven and eight o'clock in the morning, I saw a female come round the corner, and the prosecutor came after her—he was very drunk—the female ran into a passage—Seager was not there at that time.
COURT. Q. How long have you lived at No. 1? A. About nine months—I know O'Brien—I have sent for a pint of beer there, and have been in the house—he comes and is shaved at my shop—he keeps a beer-shop—I do not know whether Seager lodges there—it is a lodging-house for single men—I only know Seager by coming to my shop to be shaved—I am not much friends with him.
CHARLES TOOL . I live at No. 6, George-street. I went into O'Brien's house about half-past seven o'clock that morning, and saw Seager there—he appeared tipsy, and O'Brien refused to serve him—I went next door, and in about five minutes I heard a disturbance in the street—I went out, and saw Seager in custody.
COURT. Q. Who lives next door to O'Brien? A. I do—I am a shoemaker—I know Mr. O'Brien, and some of the persons who lodge in his house—I do not see any of them here.
ELIZA SMITH . I live with my mother. On the morning of the 2nd of August I was going to work, at a little after seven o'clock—I was thirsty, and called in at the public-house at the corner of Plumtree-street—I saw the prosecutor, very drunk, with three females—he staggered and fell—one of the
females pulled him up, placed him against a barrel, and told him to stand there—one of them winked at the other who had no bonnet on, to come out—they came out—one went towards George-street, and two towards Holborn—the prosecutor went towards George-street, after the woman who went there—neither of the prisoners were there.
COURT. Q. How long have you known Seager? A. I do not know him—I did not see him there.
Lloyd's Defence. I was going home and speaking to a young friend, when they took me up—the prosecutor came and said in the station, if I would give him two sovereigns he would not prosecute; if not, he would go as far as the law would allow him.
(—Cole, of Well-street; William Seard, of Hart-street;—Coleman, of King-street, Corent-garden; and—Harraden, a tailor; gave Seager a good character.)
LLOYD — GUILTY . Aged 27.—
SEAGER — GUILTY . Aged 22.—
Transported for Ten Years.

JAMES FOREMAN . The prisoner lodged with me—I one day found a for of the prisoner's which fitted my kitchen—I went to the pawnbroker's and found this parasol which is mine.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Show me your mark on it? A. I have another at home to match it—this is not the colour it was when I lost it—it has been dipped since—it was a light drab one—I know it by tie handle, and the size and every thing about it.
GEORGE FINNIGAN . I am a pawnbroker. This parasol was pledged I believe by the prisoner—the silk has been dyed.
Cross-examined. Q. I suppose it is not the first parasol you hare seen that has been dyed? A. No—I can undertake to say that this has been dyed from a light colour to a dark one—here is a place where it has not taken the dye.
COURT. Q. Could they have dyed the silk without taking it to pieces? A. I do not know.
NOT GUILTY .

2499. ROBERT LANHAM, JOHN MANN , and MARY MANN were indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of Aug., 5 10 l. and 6 5 l. Bank notes, the monies of Edward Roach, from his person.
EDWARD ROACH . I am a dealer. I lost my watch, and was going about—Lanham and John Mann came up to me, and told me not to be frightened, it was all right, they would assist me to find my watch—I took them into a public-house in Essex-street, and called for some beer—I laid my head on the table, and went to sleep—the landlord awoke me, and asked me if my money was right—I felt, and missed from my pocket 80 l. in Bank of England notes, which I had received from Barnetts and Co., for a check for 100 l.
MARTHA EDMONDS . I am the wife of Thomas Edmonds, and live at the Catherine Wheel, in Essex-street. The prosecutor came in, and fell asleep—the three prisoners were there—Lanham put his hand into the prosecutor's pocket, and took something out—he wanted to escape, but my husband would not let him go—John Mann appeared to be asleep on the other side of the prosecutor, and Mary was by his side—I said something to them, and Mary Mann and Lanham said they were all friends together—Mary Mann whispered to Lanham a great deal—Mary and Lanham wanted to go, but I would not let them—Lanham then went into the yard—my husband followed him—he left the house, and my husband fetched him back with the notes in his pocket.
JOHN THOMAS EDMONDS . When I got home my wife told me something—I went into the tap-room—Lanham was on one side of the prosecutor, and John Mann on the other—I saw the prosecutor's pocket right up—I seised Lanham, and said, "What are you about? I will suffer nothing wrong in my house"—Lanham said, "We are all friends, let this man have his nap out"—I said, "Let him have it, but you shan't go"—Lanham went into the yard—I followed him—I awoke the prosecutor, and said, "Search and see what you have lost"—he said, "I have got a 5 l. note changed," and he had got some silver and gold—I then said the prisoners might go, I begged their pardon—I then heard the prosecutor cry out, "I have lost 80 l."—Lanham had got out—I ran out, and challenged him with not paying for the beer—he came back, and at that moment I turned the key, and sent for a policeman—he was searched, and 80 l. in notes found in his breeches' pocket.
Lanham. I took the notes out of my pocket. Witness. Yes, I took them directly; he said they were counterfeits.
EDWARD ROACH re-examined. I never knew the prisoners before.
John Mann's Defence. I was fast asleep.
LANHAM — GUILTY . Aged 54.
J. MANN — GUILTY . Aged 31.
M. MANN — GUILTY . Aged 25.
Confined One Year.

2502. JAMES O'NEIL and JAMES JOHN FITZGERALD were indicted for stealing, on the 21st of Aug., 48 pairs of boots, value 5 l., the goods of Edward Wright; in a certain vessel in a port of entry and discharge.
JAMES CROW . I am mate of the Leven Lass, which is lying at Poplar; Edward Wright is the captain. On the 17th of Auga 1 received a trunk on board—I left the vessel at half-past five o'clock in the evening of the 25th of Aug., O'Neil was on board—I came back, and was on board—the dog barked in the night—I came on deck, and found Fitzgerald there—he is a seaman, and O'Neil was an apprentice—I have missed these boots—they are part of the cargo, and were in this trunk—they were picked up alongside the vessel.
HENRY SKIPP . I went on board, and informed the captain that a trunk of shoes had been picked up afloat in the dock—he called O'Neil, and asked him some questions, and O'Neil, said he knew all about it—I went down stairs with him—O'Neil gave me the key of his box—I unlocked it, and took twenty-six pair of these boots—there were eight dozen pair of boots in the box that was floating about—Fitzgerald was in bed—O'Neil said in his presence that he had given them him to put into his box—Fitzgerald denied it, and said before the Magistrate that he knew O'Neil had put them into his box, but he did not give them to him.
ROBERT TAYLOR . I am an officer—I said, "I have picked up eight dozen pair of boots, and what has become of the others?—O'Neil said, "They are in the forecastle"—we opened the trunk with the key that O'Neil gave up—I took out twenty-four pairs, and the other officer took out twenty-four pairs—Fitz-gerald said, "I wish I had been dead hefore this had happened."
MOSS HART . I sent these boots and shoes to the ship, under the prosecutor's care.
O'Neil. The dog made a noise, Fitzgerald came to me in my bed, and asked if my chest was open, I said it was—next morning I opened it, and saw parcels made up in it—I said I would keep them till I knew where they came from—I intended to ask the captain about them—if I had known they were stolen I would not have left them in it.
JAMES CROW re-examined. Any one on board had access to these trunks—I found Fitzgerald on the fore-part of the vessel—he had no business there that I know of—I asked what was the matter—he said he was taken suddenly ill.
NOT GUILTY .
Sixth Jury, before Edward Bullock, Esq.
