2504. HENRY BAKER, ROBERT BAKER, ANN JOHNSON , and CAROLINE WILLIAMS , were indicted for stealing, on the 8th of Aug., 4 sovereigns, the monies of Jonathan Davies, from his person.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
JONATHAN DAVIES . I am a labourer, and live in Windsor-court, Falcon-square. On the 8th of Aug. I had just arrived from Canada—I had 16 l. 3 s. in money—I met with Bowden, and went with him to a public-house—I then went to buy a hat—I parted with Bowden in Finsbury-square, and took a cab in Aldgate, which Henry Baker drove—I told him to drive to Falcon-square—I got to the Lock and Key, in Smithfield—I did not tell the prisoner to stop there—I had something to drink—I was sober when I left Bowden—Henry Baker brought some beer to the door of the cab, and I drank some—I was not quite sober when I got to the Lock and Key—I showed the money to the landlady—I believed I put it again into my pocket-book—next morning I found myself at the police-station, and somebody had taken all my money.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Do you remember taking Johnson and Williams into the cab? A. No—I parted with Bowden about eight o'clock—I will not say that I did not stop the cab, and get out myself into the public-house—I bad sixteen sovereigns and 3 s. at first, and I bought a hat, which cost 8 s.
JOHN BOWDEN . I am employed in the London Docks. I have known the prosecutor four years—I met him about four o'clock in the afternoon—he was sober—we went to the Bricklayers' Arms, in Back Church-lane—we had three pots of half-and-half between three of us—I took him to the hatter's, and there I first saw his money—I should say he had from ten to fourteen sovereigns—he opened his pocket-book to take one out to pay for his hat—we went to Aldgate, and took a cab, which H. Baker drove to Clifton-street, Finsbury-market, and I got out there—I left the prosecutor, and for 6 d. more H. Baker was to drive him to Falcon-square—when I left him his money was safe that I had seen at the hatter's—he was sober—this was at half-past seven.
MARIA WILD . My father keeps the Lock and Key in Smithfield. On the evening of the 8th of August the prosecutor came with a cab -man about nine o'clock—there was a little boy on the box—they stood at the bar, and had some half-and-half and two glasses of gin—the prosecutor threw some sovereigns on the counter—I do not know how many—he was not sober—H. Baker asked me to persuade him to put his money into his pocket-book—he did so—they then took their half-and-half into the tap-room.
Cross-examined. Q. He threw his money about like a drunken man? A. Yes—there was a gentleman at the counter, but no ladies—I did not see him put his pocket-book into his pocket.
ABRAHAM LAZARUS . I am a dealer in unredeemed pledges, and live in Tenter-street, Spitalfields. About half-past ten o'clock on this night I was in the Vinegar-ground—I gaw a cab driving very furiously—it nearly knocked me down—Henry Baker drove, and Robert was on the box with him—I said, "Can't you mind where you are driving?"—he made some answer, which I did not hear—he drove on—the cab came up Vincent-street, and turned round Catherine-street—it stopped there—both the men got down from the box—Henry opened the door, and Robert laid hold of something, which appeared to me to be a bundle, but which turned out to be the prosecutor—he was bundled out into the road—Johnson and Williams were in the cab —Johnson had a black bonnet on, and Williams had a straw bonnet on—Johnson was pushing the prosecutor out—Williams was coming out of the cab, and was shoved back by Johnson—the cab drove off—I halloed out, "Pick up your bundle"—they went on—I went up, and found it was the prosecutor in a helpless state—I gave directions, and the cab was brought back.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. How near were you to the cab? A. About ten yards—I do not speak to the faces of Johnson or Williams, only to their bonnets.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Do you buy goods of pawnbrokers? A. I do—I have never acted as an attorney for prisoners in my life—I might have given a brief over for prisoners as their friend, not deriving any profit—I did not know them before—they were no friends of mine—I did it for nothing—I was tried seven years ago for a bedstead which I bought—to the best of my belief there was three sovereigns and some silver lying about on the pavement—when the cry was made the neighbours came out—I am not living in a brothel, nor constantly in brothels—I was looking for a lady that night.
JAMES DAWSON . I am a cabinet-maker, and live in Catherine-street. About half-past ten o'clock that night I was standing at my door—I saw the cab stop, and, after a time, drive off again as hard as it could drive—some one who was with me attracted my attention—I went where the cab stopped, and found the prosecutor.
JAMES FARRELL ( police-constable N 145.) About eleven o'clock this night I saw the cab in the Vinegar-ground—Henry and Robert Baker were on the box—Johnson and Williams were inside—it was going very fast—we were called to stop it—I turned round the corner, and saw the prosecutor—I looked on the ground, and found three sovereigns and a half-crown—Lazarus called my attention to the money—I got the prosecutor into the cab, and took charge of Johnson and Williams, who were in the cab —another officer got oh the box—Williams began crying and wringing her hands, and said several times she knew she should be transported now—I was supporting the prosecutor's head, and noticed Johnson stoop down—her hand slipped along my leg—when I got to the station I got a light, and found one sovereign, two half-sovereigns, 2 s. and one halfpenny in the cab —I searched Robert Baker, and found 9 s. 2 d. on him.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. You found nothing on Williams? A. No.
JAMES CAFFRAY ( police-constable N 153.) I was in Old-street Road, and heard the cry of "Policeman, stop the cab "—I looked round, and saw the cab coming furiously towards me—I ran and stopped it—I took H. and R. Baker into custody—I went back, and found the prosecutor, took him into the cab, and then I got on the box—while I was going along I asked Henry where he got the prosecutor—he said he took him in Aldgate, drove him to Finsbury, then to the Lock and Key, back to Aldgate, then to the City-road
and the Vinegar-ground—I asked if he had paid him his fare—he said not—I asked why he put him down there—he said he did not know—I asked who would pay him his fare—he said he should look to the girls for it, that the prosecutor took the girls in at the City-road—I searched him at the station, and found on him 9 s. 4 1/4 d.—I saw the money picked up where the cab stopped—the prisoners were all of them sober—it was an hour and three-quarters before the prosecutor was quite recovered—I found this pocket-book, with the strap broken, 5 l. 13 s. 6 1/2 d. in it, and a small American coin, on him—on my way to the station I saw a half-crown on the ground—I picked up a half-crown and a halfpenny.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18430821-2505]
2505. WILLIAM OLIVER was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of July, 3 pieces of paper, called pawnbroker's tickets, value 15 s. 6 d.; the property of William Dean his master.
WILLIAM DEAN . I am a silk weaver, and live in Gascoine-place, Bethnal-green—the prisoner used to work where I did—on Saturday night, the 22nd of July, I had three pawnbroker's duplicates safe in a china coffee strainer in my room—one was for a watch pledged for 5 s., and the others for a coat for 8 s., and two silk handkerchiefs for 2 s. 6 d.—I got them from Mr. Miller in the Kingsland-road—the prisoner was with me about seven days—he was with me on the Monday after I saw these things safe—he left me about seven o'clock on Tuesday night, and I missed the tickets about a quarter after seven o'clock—I went to stop them, and saw him in the Hackney-road as I came back—the pawnbroker told me something—I saw the prisoner again about half-past eight o'clock peeping round the corner by my house—he saw me and ran away—I ran after him, and a policeman stopped him.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. You did not wish to prosecute? A. No; I have not said if the pawnbroker would give me back my pledges I would not prosecute.
FRANCIS LEATHERS . I am assistant to Thomas Miller, a pawnbroker in the Kingsland-road—these things were pawned by the prosecutor and his wife—on Tuesday the 25th of July the prisoner brought two tickets of a watch and a coat—he said he wanted more on them—I sent for the things down and looked at them and refused to give him more—the witness came and I told him.
Cross-examined. Q. Are you sure the prisoner is the man? A. Yes.
ABRAHAM GUNTON ( police-constable.) I took the prisoner—he said he threw the tickets away,
GUILTY. Aged 21.— Recommended to Mercy. — Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18430821-2506]
2506. JULIA SIMMONDS was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of August, 1 breast-pin, value 5 s., the goods of Edward Roach, from his person.
EDWARD ROACH . I live in Old North-street, Red Lion-square. About one in the morning of the 25th of August I was in Falcon-street—the prisoner caught hold of me by the coat and wished me to give her something—I desired her to go away, and went into the road to avoid her—I left her, and as I passed round into Aldersgate-street I put my hand on my breast and my pin was gone—it was there a quarter of an hour before—I had stopped with no one else—as I turned I saw a policeman—he went and took her—this is my pin.
Cross-examined by MR. CHARNOCK. Q. I suppose you were perfectly sober? A. Yes—I did not stand with her one minute—I had not promised her 2 s. or 2 s. 6 d., or any sum—I told her to go away.
JAMES PARKER ( City police-constable, No. 257.) About one o'clock on this morning I was passing Falcon-street—I saw the prisoner and the prosecutor—the prosecutor was putting out his hand, telling her to keep away from him—he crossed the street, and crossed again, telling her to keep off-—she went towards Goswell-street, and the prosecutor towards the Post-office—I told her if I saw her do that again I would take her up—the prosecutor then spoke to me—I went and spoke to the prisoner about the pin—she was in the act of taking it from her shawl—as soon as she found I felt it, she said, "Here it is"—gbe begged and prayed he would not prosecute her.
GUILTY . Aged 38.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18430821-2507]
2507. WILLIAM HOWELL was indicted for stealing, on the 18th of August, 1 purse, value 2 s. 6 d.; 4 sovereigns, and 2 shillings; the property of Selina Elizabeth Nicholls, from her person.
SELINA ELIZABETH NICHOLLS . I am a widow, and live in Hart-street, Covent-garden. On the 18th of August, about one o'clock, I was in James-street, Covent-garden—I crossed from Long-acre—I was going into my father's house—a person named Jordan was going in just after, and told me something—I felt in my right-side pocket, and missed my purse, containing four sovereigns and a half-crown—it was safe at nine o'clock in the morning—I had not used it in the mean time—I have not seen it since.
NAOMI JORDAN . I am ten years old, and live in Henrietta-street. About half-past twelve o'clock, on the 18th of August, I was in Long-acre—I saw the prisoner and three men behind the prosecutrix—a man in a white hat put his hand into the prosecutrix's pocket, and took a purse out—the prisoner, took it from him, and put it into his side coat-pocket—he then walked fast up Hart-street, as if he was going to Bow-street—I went into the shop, and laid to the prosecutrix, "You have lost your purse"—she said, "No, I have not got my pocket on"—she felt, and said she had got her pocket on, and had lost her purse
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Which way Were you coming? A. From St. Martin's-lane—I saw the three men for about five minutes—the one in the white hat said to the others, "You two go on the other side of the way, I can do it"—he took it, put up his finger, and called them over to him, and then they all went away—I am sure the prisoner was one—I saw him again at Bow-street about half an hour after.
WILLIAM HALL . I am a green-grocer, and live in Kmg-sireet, Long-acre. On this day 1 saw five persons about together in James-street—I had seen them frequently before—the prisoner was one of them—I saw something taken from the prosecutrix's pocket by one of them, and given to one of the others, I do not know which of them—they were all close together.
Cross-examined. Q. When the prosecutrix's pocket was picked were they all five together? A. No, only three one on one side, and two on the other—the three were all close together till after the pocket was picked—I cannot be mistaken—I saw the prisoner at Bow-street, and did not know him at first—when I saw his hat on, the second time I did know him—I was called to look two or three times, and said I did not think he was one of them, for some time—we all went out of the office together, and in a quarter of an hour after I said I thought I knew him—I am quite sure I am right about the five men being on one side of the way, three on one side of the lady, and two on the other—the Magistrate did not tell me I was committing perjury, that I recollect—I had some doubt of the prisoner till I saw him with a hat on.
GEORGE ANDERSON ( police-constable C 85.) I received information, and
apprehended the prisoner in Covent-garden market—there was another man with him—he came out of James-street, and crossed into the market.
Cross-examined. Q. Who gave you information? A. The foreman to the prosecutrix's father—the prisoner had this coat on his arm—he was dressed just as he is now—2 s. 2 1/2 d. was found on him.
NOT GUILTY .

2509. WILLIAM LEE was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of Feb., 1 truck, value 2 l. 10 s., the goods of Walter Walker.
FREDERICK CLEVELAND . In Feb. last I was in the service of Walter Walker, a porter, in Rathbone-place, Oxford-street—he lets out trucks. The prisoner came to hire a truck for an hour or an hour and a half, and said he was going to Somers-town—I let him one at 3 d. an hour—he took it away, and did not bring it back—I did not see him again till last Friday at Bow-street.
Prisoner. You have known me for many years. Witness. Yes, and know no harm of you.
WALTER WALKER . I am a porter, and live in Rathbone-place. The prisoner has hired trucks of me by the hour—I do not recollect this truck being let to him—I found I had one less—I never got it back again.
Prisoner. I took it back at eleven o'clock at night—at Bow-street he said, "You left it in the street,"—you were drunk, and did not know what became of it.
Prisoner's Defence. There were more trucks there; I took the worst.
GUILTY. Aged 40.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury. — Confined
Six Weeks.
(There was another indictment against the prisoner.)

2510. CAROLINE COX was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of Aug., 1 frock, value 4 s.; 2 shawls, 1 l. 8 s.; 3 paintings, 1 l.; 2 shifts, 10 s.; 1 printed book, 12 s.; 1 book, 1 l.; 1 box, 1 s.; 1 toilet cover, 3 s.; 1 gown, 6 d.; 1 bonnet, 6 d.; 1 collar, 2 s.; 3 handkerchiefs, 6 s.; 6 shillings, 4 sixpences, and 4 groats; the property of Sarah Dryden: and 1 coat, 2 l.; 1 sheet, 6 s.; and 1 shirt, 4 s.; the goods of James Dryden, her master.
SARAH DRYDEN . I am the daughter of James Dryden. The prisoner was in my father's service—when I came home I missed a quantity of things—the prisoner's sister gave a quantity of duplicates to my father, and he gave them to me—these are my things and my father's—he is ill.
WILLIAM COTTER . I am assistant to Francis Ogleby, a pawnbroker, in High-street, Poplar. I produce a frock pawned for 2 s. by a girl, who gave the name of Church—this is the duplicate of it—it was not pawned by the prisoner.
GEORGE WOODFORD . I am assistant to Mr. Hawkins, a pawnbroker, in Catherine-street, Limehouse. I produce a sheet.
HENRY JEROME . I am a pawnbroker. I produce a shawl pawned for 3 s. and a shirt for 9 d. by the prisoner—these are the duplicates I gave her.
Prisoner's Defence. I gave the duplicates of all these things to Mr. Dryden—he said if I did so he would get the things out.
GUILTY. Aged 17.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Three Months.

2511. ANN HEWITT and SARAH BENNETT were indicted for stealing, on the 24th of Aug., 1 petticoat, value 1 s. 6 d., the goods of Robert Watt; and that Hewitt had before been convicted of felony; to which
BENNETT pleaded GUILTY .— Transported for Seven Years.
MARIA STEVENS . I live in Clerkenwell. On the 24th of Aug. I was passing the prosecutor's in Exmouth-street, and I saw the prisoners together—I saw Hewitt take a petticoat from the side of the rail at the side of the shop, and put it under her shawl—they both walked away—I gave information—they were taken, and the petticoat found on them.
Hewitt. Q. Did you not say at first it was Bennett took it, and afterwards say it was me? A. No.
JAMES HARMAN . I live with Robert Watt, in Exmouth-street. I received information, and ran after the prisoners—when I got close to them Hewitt took the petticoat from under her shawl, and threw it in at a door way—this is my master's.
RICHARD BYRNE ( police-constable E 116.) I produce a certificate of Hewitt's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—the prisoner is the person.
HEWITT — GUILTY .— Transported for Seven Years.

2514. ELIZA NIGHTINGALE BROWN was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of June, 4 blankets, value 2 l.; 2 sheets, 14 s.; 1 snuff-box, 6 d.; and 1 printed book, 1 d.; the goods of John Forrester, her master: and ELIZA BASSETT BROWN , for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen.
JOHN FORRESTER . I live in Compton-street. The prisoner Nightingale worked for me—I missed a sheet from the bed on which she slept, and the blanket from the bed underneath—I asked her where they were—she made no reply—I pressed for an answer, and then she said she took them to her mother's to wash.
Cross-examined by MR. DOWLING. Q. Did you say it would be better to tell the truth? A. No—she was never required to wash the bedding of her own bed—this snuff-box and book are mine—they are worth about 7 d.
THOMAS HOOKER . I am a pawnbroker—I produce a sheet and blanket—I took in the blanket of Bassett Brown.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. She had pawned a great number of things, and taken them out? A. Yes—I have known her about twelve months.
ROBERT ADAMS ( police-constable E 109.) I took Nightingale Brown, and found in her box the snuff-box and book—I went to Elizabeth Bassett Brown, her mother next morning, and said I came for the blanket and sheet her daughter had sent to be washed—she said she had not got them—I said, "Where are they?"—she said they were pledged—she went up stairs, and came down with these two tickets and some others—she said she hoped her daughter had not stolen them—I said, "You knew they were not yours"—she said, "Yes—I knew they were stolen."
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. How long was it before she said they were stolen? A. It might have been ten minutes.
COURT. Q. May she not have said, "I did not know they were stolen?" A. No, she said the other.
Cross-examined by MR. DOWLING. Q. Was Nightingale Brown's box locked? A. No.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18430821-2515]
2515. WILLIAM HENRY HENLY was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of August, 2 pairs of trowsers, value 2 s. 6 d.; and one gown, 7 s. 6 d.; the goods of William Henly.
WILLIAM HENLY . The prisoner is my son; he had run away from home—I missed a pair of his trowsers—I then went to town, and could not find him—when I returned, his mother missed this gown and two pairs of trowsers—these now produced are mine—I found a duplicate on the prisoner, which led me to the pawnbroker's.
HENRY EDWARD BONHAM . I produce this gown—I took it in pledge of a woman—this is the duplicate I gave for it.
Prisoner. I am very sorry for it.
GUILTY. Aged 13.— Judgment Respited.

EDWARD HERBERT KENNET . I live in Sussex-street, Hyde Park—about two o'clock, on the 24th of Aug., I was in St. James's Park—Wright called to me, he had my handkerchief—I had put it in my pocket in the morning, and had not used it.

[trialAccount t18430821-2520]
2520. GEORGE NICHOLSON was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of August, 1 jacket, value 1 l.; the goods of Charles Buckman.
CHARLES BUCKMAN . I am a sailor, and live in Hoxton Old-town. On the 15th of August I left my jacket on the table where I lodged—I missed it the same evening—I went to the Britannia saloon in search of the prisoner the next day, and accused him of stealing it—he said if I would keep him from trouble and out of the policeman's hand, he would make the jacket right to me, and told me to go further up the street that the crowd should not know what was the matter—I walked up about half a mile, then stopped, and asked him about the jacket—he began at high words—the constable came up and asked what was the matter—he said there was nothing the matter—he then told me if I went up a little further he would tell me all—the jacket has never been found—after I gave him into the hands of the police he told something.
HARRIET TAYLOR . The prisoner came into my room and asked if the prosecutor, who is my brother, was at home—I said no—he took a book and began to read—I went into the next room—he was in the room two hoars—I afterwards heard him go—I then went into the room and missed the jacket off the table—there was no one in the room but my little sister—if any one else had come in I should have heard them.
LOUISA TAYLOR . I remember the prisoner coming on the 15th of August—he was there two hours—I went out of the room for two minutes, and when I came back the jacket was gone—I had put the book which I had been reading, on the jacket on the table—the book was still left—this was the prosecutor's jacket.
GUILTY . Aged 21.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18430821-2521]
2521. THOMAS JAMES GREEN was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of August, 1 shoe, value 2 s.; and one stocking, value 8 d.; the goods of William Clark.
WILLIAM CLARK . I live with my father, James Clark—I Im in a school—my father does not find my shoes and stockings—on the 19th of August I took off my stocking and shoe on Bow Common—I put them down to go and play—the prisoner ran away with them—I ran after him—he got away, and I told the policeman—I am sure he is the boy—I had not known him before.
GEORGE OSBORNE ( police-constable K. 129) Clark came to me crying—I ran and took the prisoner—ho said, "I have not got it, another boy ran away with it"—I took him back to the prosecutor, who said he was positive he was the boy.
Prisoner's Defence. A boy with me took them, and I ran away.
GUILTY . Aged 14.— Confined Two Months.
[trialAccount t18430821-2522]
2522. MARY MURRAY was indicted for stealing, on thr 29th of July, 2 petticoats, value 6 s.; 2 shifts, 1 s. 3 d.; and 3 gowns; the goods of Henry Phillips Arnold, from the person of William Arnold.
ELIZABETH ARNOLD . I am the wife of Henry Phillips Arnold. On the 29th of July I sent a basket, containing two petticoats, three gowns, and two shifts, by my little boy, to Lamb's Conduit-street—he brought the basket back without the linen—I have not found the things.
WILLIAM ARNOLD . On the 29th of July my mother sent me with a basket of clothes—as I was going along the prisoner stopped me, and said she was left in care of the house, and I was to leave them with her—in consequence of that I went into a house, and left them there—I am quite sure she is the woman.
HENRY ARNOLD . I am brother of William Arnold—the prisoner is the woman that took the things.
Prisoner's Defence. I was in Lamb's Conduit-street—some boys were tormenting me—I met a woman and asked where she was going to—she said to No. 5—I took the child to Museum-street.
GUILTY .* Aged 25.— Transported for Seven Years.
There were two other indictments against the prisoner.
[trialAccount t18430821-2523]
2523. JOHN LUMSDEN and ALFRED BASHFORD were indicted for stealing, on the 10th of July, 1 watch, value 1 l. 10 s.; 1 scarf, 2 s. 6 d.; 1 necklace, 1 s.; 1 sovereign; and 23 shillings; the property of Thomas Bailey.
ELIZABETH BAILEY . On the 10th of July the prisoners came to my house about ten o'clock—Bashford was a stranger—they sat in the kitchen a bit—a, man named Howe came to mend a mantel-piece—Lumsden said, "Mrs. Bailey, send the old man out for some beer"—I said, "There is no cause to spend your money"—he would persist in it, and put sixpence, a penny, and two halfpence in my hand—I sent Howe for the beer, and went up and let him in—I showed them all over the house, and while Lumsden kept me talking in the kitchen, Bashford bolted with my husband's watch, which I had seen safe at twelve o'clock over the fire-place—I did not miss it till I sat down to dinner—this is it—it is No. 6394.
Lumsden. I had a friend with me, but it was not Bashford. Witness. I am convinced it was Bashford.
HENRY BILSON . I am assistant to Mr. Barker, a pawnbroker, in Hounds-ditch—this watch was pledged by Bashford on the 10th of July—I think about two or three o'clock—I live five miles from the prosecutrix.
WILLIAM ANGUS . I am a stonemason, and live in Essex-street, Commercial-road. I know the prisoners—on the 7th of July I saw them in the Mile-end-road—I played at skittles with them at the Maid and Magpie in Stepney, but I never saw them speak to each other—I believe they came in company—I owed Lumsden 1 s.—he came to me in the Star and Garter for it, and asked if I was going to the Prince of Wales, and there I saw Bashford.
HENRY FRANCES ( police-constable N 227.) I went after Lumsden—I told him I took him on suspicion of stealing Mrs. Bailey's watch—on the way to the Court, next day, he asked if she missed anything—I said, "No"—I took
Bashford next day—he said he came from Lee, in Kent—I found this duplicate on him—I heard both say they never saw each other before.
(Lumsden received a good character.)
BASHFORD — GUILTY . Aged 22.— Confined Six Months.
LUMSDEN — GUILTY . Aged 28.

2524. JOHN LUMSDEN was again indicted for stealing, on the 13th of June, 2 marble chimney-pieces, value 14 l. 14 s., the goods of James Rogers, and another.
JOHN CHRISTIE . I live in Lower Queen-street, Rotherhithe, and am in partnership with James Rogers. On the 13th of June I missed two chimney pieces from Princes-street, Rotherhithe—they were worth fourteen guineas—these are them.
JOSEPH BENJAMIN AVILA . I am a pawnbroker, and live in the Mile End-road. These two chimney-pieces were pawned on the 17th of Aug., by the prisoner, for 2 l.—I have no doubt about it—I gave the money to him.
Prisoner. I was in Whitechapel-road—Jackson called me, and asked me to lend him a hand to shove the truck down to the turnpike, and he would stand two or three pots of beer—I said, "What have you got?" he said, "Marble chimney-pieces to pawn, at Mrs. Avila's"—I lent him a hand, and he pawned them.
GUILTY . Aged 28.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18430821-2525]
2525. MARGARET ALLEN was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of Aug., 5 flat-irons, value 5 s.; 1 tea-kettle, 9 s.; 1 purse, 6 d.; 7 sovereigns, and 1 half-sovereign, the property of Sophia Woodden, her mistress.
SOPHIA WOODDEN . The prisoner lived with me for a month as a weekly servant. On the 12th of Aug. I put my purse behind my pillow—it contained seven sovereigns and a half—I missed them after breakfast next morning—she was very poor indeed when she came to me—I lent her 1 s. on the Friday to buy a second-hand pair of shoes, as all her shoes were gone.
JAMES BRADLEY ( police-constable B 134.) I took the prisoner on the Friday following the robbery, and asked if she had got any money—she said she had none—she had a bonnet-box at her lodging, in Welborough-place, Chelsea—I found two boxes there, filled with good articles, which appeared to have been released from pawn, and in the large chest which she had not told me of, I found four sovereigns.
ELIZABETH KNOWLES . The prisoner came to lodge with me at Mrs. Foley's—I did not see her bring the boxes—I did not say that the boxes searched by the policeman, were brought there by her—I believe them to be hers.
ELIZABETH ARNOLD . I am searcher at the station. The prisoner told me the had got no money—I found on her 9 s. 2 d.—she asked me what would be done with her—I said I did not know—she said seven sovereigns and a half of the old lady's were lost, but they would not find the purse—if they went to her lodging they would find some money.
MARY ANN GRAHAM . I was at Mr. Perkins's, the pawnbroker's, on the 16th of Aug.—I saw the prisoner there—she gave some tickets in—I saw her change a sovereign, and take two parcels out, and then I saw her give another sovereign.
Prisoner. I had but one sovereign in my hand. Witness. You changed one, and when I came back you changed another—I said, "You have made a good harvest to leave the wash-tub."
Prisoner's Defence. I got four sovereigns at a raffle, and the rest I worked
hard for; on the Tuesday morning the prosecutrix sent me to the butcher's, to see if she had left her purse there.
GUILTY . Aged 30.— Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18430821-2526]
2526. DENNIS M'CARTHY was indicted for receiving, on the 15th of Aug., 6 balls of hemp, value 2 s.; 1/4 lb. weight of nails, 2 d.; 3 leather backs for boots, 2 s.; 2 leather fronts for shoes, 1 s.; and 1lb. weight of leather, 8 d.; the goods of Henry Snead, of a certain ill-disposed person, well knowing them to be stolen.
WILLIAM FREEMAN . I am shopman to Mr. Henry Snead, and live in Tothill-street, Westminster—Took has been in the service—about ten o'clock on the 15th of August, I found a pair of soles in his trowsers when he was going home—I went with him to a house in Bedfordbury—the prisoner lives there—he opened the door—I went into a room on the first floor—the prisoner said it was his room—it was searched, and six balls of hemp, five soles, some nails, three boot-backs, and some other things found—I believe them to be my master's, but would not swear to them—the policeman continued the search, and was tying up these things—the prisoner said, "You have no occasion to search any further, the devil a farthing's worth more is there in the place than what you have got"—I never saw him at my master's.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. You only know these from circumstances? A. Yes—I cannot say when I had seen them safe—8 d. is not much under the value of the soles to sell, but if I went to buy them, I should have to pay 14 d. or 16 d. for them.
ROBERT TOOK . I am thirteen years old. I have been in the prosecutor's service—the prisoner met me one day in New-street, Covent-garden—he said, "Can you get me a bit of leather to mend my shoes with?"—I said, "I will get you a bit if I can"—he said, "Don't let any one see you"—next day I gave him a pair of soles—he gave me 8 d. for them—I then brought him a half sole—he gave me 4 d. for it, and said, "Try to bring me some more if you can"—I did so, and he gave me 6 d.—he repeatedly told me to get in re—all these things are Mr. Snead's property.
Cross-examined. Q. Did not he refuse to take goods of you? A. No—I met him by the Boccius lamp—he kept a stall there—he knew my father was a shoemaker, and lived up a court there.
WILLIAM CHAPLEN ( police-constable B 118.) I found these things in the room.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 43.— Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18430821-2527]
2527. WILLIAM NOCK was indicted for embezzlement.
WILLIAM PRITCHARD . I live in High-street, Poplar—the prisoner was my shopman—when he sold things he ought to account to me directly, and enter it in a book—I was in the shop on the 21st of Aug.—he left about eight o'clock that morning—there is no entry of 7 s. 6 d. that day—nor have I had it.
Prisoner. I entered his service not on a salary, but to have ten per cent on the profits, which made me consider myself a sort of partner—and he agreed I should take the ten per cent, every week—a few weeks after I said I did not want it every week, and he authorized me, if I wanted money to take it, and put it down in the book. Witness. He went away directly he bad received this—he came back on the Wednesday evening drunk—I said, "About this 7 s. 6 d."—he said, "It is all right, I always keep these clear"—I did not give him authority to take money—I had given him notice to leave, but his time had not expired.
WILLIAM SHAW . I went to the prosecutor's shop, between seven and eight o'clock in the morning of the 21st of August—I bought something and paid the prisoner 7 s. 6 d.
GUILTY . Aged 27.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18430821-2528]
2528. THOMAS CANEY was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of December, 7 printed books, value 14 s., the goods of Elizabeth Tomlin.
ELIZABETH TOMLIN . I keep a circulating library in Merlin-place, Clerkenwell—the prisoner came to me on the 10th of Dec, and had seven volumes.
Cross-examined by MR. JONES. Q. Is Mrs. Scriven your sister? A. Yes—they are my books, and the premises are hers—I am single—I never saw the prisoner before that day—persons pay according to the time they keep the books—there was no time mentioned to return them that I recollect.
COURT. Q. What is the usual time? A. A week or a fortnight—we lend them for six or seven months, if they are going to take them into the country—he never told me they were for the country—they have not been returned.
JANE SCRIVEN . The prisoner came for. the books on the 10th of Dec.—I am sure he is the man.
ANN GUNN . I am the wife of Mr. Gunn—I remember seeing the prisoner that day—I have no doubt about of him.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you seen him before? A. Yes, two or three times in Clerken well.
JURY to ELIZABETH TOMLIN. In what name did he take the books? A. Mr. Scott—there was no deposit paid for them.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY .— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18430821-2529]
2529. FERDINAND VALENTINE was indicted for stealing, on the 22nd of May, 1 guitar, value 10 s.; 3 time-pieces, 3 l.; 4 handkerchiefs, 8 s.; 3 brushes, 6 s.; 1 pair of scissors, 1 s.; 2 stocks, 3 s.; 1 pair of drawers, 1 s.; 3 accordions, 1 l. 10 s.; 2 copper-plates, 1 l.; 2 pearl fish, 1 s.; 3 shaving-cases, 1 l.; 2 rules, 1 s.; 1 breast-pin, 6 s.; 1 shaving-brush, 1 s.; 2 waist-coats, 2 l. 10 s.; 3 pairs of trowsers, 1 l. 16 s.; 1 ring, 1 l.; 2 pattern-books, 2 s.; 2 pieces of leather, 1 s. 6 d.; 1 coat, 2 l. 1 fish-slice, 2 s., 6 d.; 1 pair of tongs, 2 s. 6 d.; and 2 books, 1 l.; the goods of Henry William Albert, his master.
MR. JONES conducted the Prosecution.
HENRY WILLIAM ALBERT . In 1841 I carried on business in London, as a merchant—I had offices in Oxford-court, Cannon-street, City. On the 19th of May I left this country—before I left, I engaged the prisoner as my clerk, at 1 l. per week—I paid him 2 l. the day before I went to France—I was away till August—in the interval some letters passed between me and him—I took a list of every thing I had in my office—I had a trunk in my counting-house, containing waistcoats, trowsers, handkerchiefs, stockings, and a ring, and I left a guitar and time-piece in my private room—I locked the trunk, and put the key into my cash-box, and the key of my cash-box I took with me—the cash-box I put into the office—I did not give the prisoner authority to open the trunk, and take any of the things, or to sell or pawn any—I returned in August, and saw the prisoner—he advised me not to go to my office, as I should be arrested for debt—I saw Duke, who was a friend of mine, and the prisoner together—I left it to them to try to arrange with my creditors—I staid in London a long time without going to my office—the prisoner came to we many times, and said, "You are watched from morning till night, and
You must not come"—I went at last, about six weeks before I had the prisoner taken—I saw my box three weeks after—it was locked—I asked my landlord to break it open—I found there was nothing left in it—all my other things were gone—I afterwards found the prisoner, and had him taken—the things I lost were worth more than 10 l.—I went with a policeman to the prisoner's house, and there found the key of my trund, and the duplicates and other things.
Cross-examined by MR. WILDE. Q. What do you deal in? A. I take goods from France—I have no office at clerks now—I had an office in Falcon-square—I paid 6 l. a quater for it—I lived there six months—I went from there to Oxford-court—I was there six years—in 1839 I was ruined, and took the benefit of the act—I then gave my name as James Henry Albert—I did not take my oath to the proceedings as Jew—I am a Protestant—I did not give my name as Jacob—when I went away I gave authority to the prisoner to carry on my business in Oxford-court, and left my office in his occupation—I paid him no money except the two weeks' salsry in advance—he was subject to expences on my behalf—I was in constant communication with him when I was at Paris—I know a person named Scalinger—Ihave seen him before with the prisoner—I have not been with him and the prisoner playing at cards—I have not been in friendly communication with the prisoner since I found this out—I know a Mr. Hill, Mr. Hobler's clerk—I have quarrel with the prisoner—I had a private quarrel at the Eight Bells—it was not about this business.
COURT. Q. Are these razors and other things yours? A. Yes; I did not find the cash-box at all—in it was the key of the trunk and a bill of Emboucheres—I know a person named Sheeler—I sas him many times before I went to Paris—I lost the key of my cash-box at paris—Sheeler was not present when I gave the prisoner the key.
MR. JONES. Q. Was your cash-box like this one? A. Yes; I cannot swear to this being it—( looking at one.)
JOHN GREAVES ( police-constable G 46.) I was called on the 3rd of July to take the prisoner—the prosecutor told him it was for robbing him of wearing apparel and other articles—the prisoner made no answer—I went with him to his lodgings in Warwick-place, Whitecross-street, and found two time-pieces, a dressing-case, a gold ring, a pin, and some other things which the prosecutor claimed—the prisoner said nothing—I also found fourteen duplicates and a key, which the prosecutor swears to as belonging to his box.
Cross-examined. Q. Were these all about the room? A. Yes; the time-piece was going at the time, over the mantel-piece.
LEONARD WILSON . I am assistant to Mr. Russell, a pawnbroker in Fore-street—nine of these duplicates were given by our house, for a pair of trowsers, three waistcoats, and a stocks, pawned on the 20th of April, 1842; two boots, pawned on the 30th of April; a tea caddy the 4th of June; a German silver fish slice and tongs on the 13th of June; two accordians on the 18th of June; and a time-piece on the 27th of June; the whole amount is 3 l. 15 s.—they were pawned by a man—I cannot say who—the prisoner has pleged things, and has renewed tickets.
HENRY WILLIAM ALBERT re-examined. This is the key of my trunk that I left in my cash-box—these things are all mine—it is possible some of them were taken from my place and pawned before I left—I never gave the prisoner authority to pledge any of these.
Cross-examined. Q. You swear to the trowsers? A. Yet; theft it no mark on them, hut I have worn them often.
MR. WILDE called
IGNATIUS DROOP . I conduct my aunt's business as a merchant—I know both the prisoner and the prosecutor—I remember the prosecutor going abroad—I lent him some money to go—he said he was going to fetch his cousin—I saw him, just previous to his going, give up the key of his cash-box to the prisoner, and tell him every thing he left in his possession, and to do the same as he did—I had a bill to indorse, and he asked the prisoner to indorse it—I said I had rather have his signature.
MR. JONES. Q. Where do you live? A. In Tower Royal, and I have a country house at Laytonstone—I have been in this country about eight years—I have never been in business for myself—the prosecutor told me that every thing the prisoner did was as if he did it himself—he told me that at Oxford-court—the prisoner was there, and there was a gentleman in his private room, with the door shut—I do not know his name—he went with the prosecutor to Paris—I have seen the key of the cash-box in the prisoner's possession since—I never saw him take the key of the trunk out of the cash-box—I saw him open the cash-box, after the prosecutor was gone, with the key that the prosecutor gave him—I went to him next day, because I forgot two bills of 150 l., and he opened the cash-box—I never saw him open the trunk—I do not know anything about opening the tea-caddy on the 4th of June—the prisoner was in my aunt's service for some months as clerk—I took him about six months after the prosecutor went—our former clerk's name was Smith—he died—I saw the prosecutor when he returned in August, and showed him the door of our office, and forbad him to come—I told him there was a person named Mullens, whom he owed money to, would take out a writ against him—I did not tell him, if he went to his office in Oxford-court, he would be arrested by a sheriff's officer—I swear that I did not tell him there was a sheriff's officer watching for him, or that it would not be safe for him to go to his office—I did not tell the prosecutor to wait patiently till bis affairs came round—he did not say he would go to bis creditors and state to them what goods he had sent from Paris to the prisoner, and what property he had left at hit office—he laid he should resign himself and his property to his creditors—I did not say I hoped he would not act unkindly by me, at he knew the business was my aunt's, and not mine—I did not tell him I would provide him with money to go to Jersey, and set up business there—I said, "I don't know what you do here; you owe me a deal of money; if you were worth anything they would take you"—he told me he would go abroad, but he did not—I have been to the prisoner's lodgings once, just to see where he lives—I have not received from him any of the prosecutor's property—the property which I received from him belonged to me—it was property which the prosecutor had bought, and I had accepted bills and paid them honestly—it was bought of a merchant at Vienna—I received property of the prisoner after the prosecutor left England—it was property which the prosecutor had bought and paid for.
MR. WILDE. Q. What does he owe you now? A. 311 l.—I am not a man of substance—I have been in business seven years and a half.
JOSEPH SULLINOTON . I am a fancy trimming-maker. When the prosecutor returned from Paris I went to meet him, as the prisoner had no money—he begged of me to do so; and as at that time of year we were very slack in business, I went to Dover—I met the prosecutor—the prisoner went first, and they came into the public-house where I was—they kept on quarrelling—I said, "I don't like to hear this, the landlord will turn us out"—the
prosecutor complained of the prisoner leaving the office—I said, "what was he to do; you left him 2 l."—he said, "That is right"—I said, "How could he keep on the office? you sent him letters and things; how could he keep on?"—he said, "Did I not leave him the trunk full of things? if he wanted he could have made money"—the prisoner said, "Of course I did; but what of that? that was not enough to keep me and pay letters and all and then I left the office"—I called at the prisoner's room on a Sunday mornning, and saw Mr. Hill there—I was not there above two minutes when the prosecutor came in—Hill began some conversation, and the prisoner said, "You had better go home to dinner"—I looked up, and said, "I don't think your clock is right"—Mrs. Valentine said, "It is not right, it was not wound up"—the prisoner took out his watch and said, "It is not right."
MR. JONES. Q. Where do you carry on business? A. In Brudenell-place, New North-road—this is my first quarter—I have got the lease of the house—I came from Shoreditch—I have been six years in this country—I have known the prisoner three or four years—he has never been in my service—he is no relation of mine—when I met the prosecutor at Dover, I did not tell him it was not safe for him to go to his counting-house—I went to Dover because the prisoner had a letter from the prosecutor to meet him—I paid the expenses—I went down with the prisoner—I said, "I will pay my expenses, but you must pay for yourself "—he said, "Mr. Albert will pay you again,"but he has not—I left them at Dover—I did not hear the prisoner tell the prosecutor it would not be safe to come to London—Valentine said, "What did you write for me to come here? you might as well have left me at home"—I then asked what was the matter, and he said the prisoner had left the office, which he has no business to do—he blowed him up for not putting on his best clothes—the prisoner said, "Don't you know I have pledged everything you left at home; but was that sufficient?"—the prosecutor said he should have managed somehow to have kept the office open.
FRANCIS HILL . I am an attorney's clerk, and have been an attorney—I have been employed by the prisoner to negociate some misunderstandings between him and the prosecutor—I had instructions to meet the prosecutor at the latter end of Feb. or beginning of March—I went to his lodgings, and saw him—he did not expect me—we had some conversation—he appointed for me to come on the following morning at a quarter before nine o'clock—I went—he said, "What have you to say?"—I said, "I think you had better settle it; you claim money of him, and he does of you; if you will meet him at Oxford-court to-morrow, and give up some letters, all will be settled"—he appointed for me to call again, and I did—he was gone out—I told him the prisoner was willing to waive his claim to his salary, if he would give up his documents and the receipt for his goods.
MR. JONES. Q. For whom did you act? A. As a friend—the prisoner had the goods either in his possession or under his control—he did not tell me he had pawned some—I did not tell the prosecutor he had pawned any—the prosecutor was looking at a list of the articles when I called on Sunday morning—he said, "These are the things that I left"—I said, "You are aware that he parted with some"—he said, "I don't know; all I want is my things back."
MR. WILDE. Q. Have you been to the prisoner's lodgings, and seen the clock over the mantel-piece? A. Yes—the prosecutor has been there—and seen the trowsers and things about.
MR. JONES called
— COLLINS. Albert rented an office of me—he went abroad in May—the prisoner discontinued his attendance there about July, at my request—I
found there was no rent, and the persons were coming for money—he was regular in his attendance till about a fortnight before he left—I broke open the trunk at the prosecutor's request.
MR. WILDE. Q. Did the prisoner conduct himself with propriety? A. Yes, to me—the whole of the furniture was mine.
GUILTY. Aged 40.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Six Months.

2530. JOHN HODDER was indicted for stealing, on the 22nd of Aug., 1 saw, value 5 s. 6 d.; the goods of Edward Hardy.
EDWARD HARDY . I live with my father, Edward Hardy. This saw was safe about six o'clock last Tuesday evening, in Hoxton market—I missed it, and ran after the prisoner, and took him with the saw on his arm, in Longalley.
WILLIAM ANDREW FERGUSON ( police-constable G 181.) A little before seven o'clock I saw a crowd—I went up—the prisoner said, "You are not wanted, it is only a lark "—the prosecutor gave him in cjiarge—in going along he said he bought it for 1 s.
Prisoner's Defence. A lad said did I want to bay a saw? I said I had only 1 s. I bought it for 1 s.
GUILTY . Aged 27.— Confined Three Months.

2531. JAMES SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 18th of Aug., 1 watch, value 5 l.; 1 seal, 10 s.; and 1 key, 10 s.; the goods of Richard Thomas Scott.
CHARLOTTE SCOTT . I am the wife of Richard Thomas Scott, and live in High-street, Poplar. About a quarter to twelve o'clock, on the 18th of Aug., I left the parlour to speak to my husband—I left a watch hanging on a nail over the chimney-piece—I was away about two minutes—when I returned the watch was gone—the prisoner was in the parlour—I asked what he wanted—he said he had paper to sell—no one could have taken it but him—he was taken in about five minutes—there were two boys with him.
Prisoner's Defence. I went with some paper; there was no one there, so I went out.
GUILTY . Aged 17.— Confined Three Months.

I saw the prisoner and another come into the shop—this merino was on a pile in the shop—the other one took it, and gave it to the prisoner, who put it under her shawl, and went off—I ran out, and brought her back with it.
Prisoner's Defence. The young woman asked me to carry it, and she would give me a trifle.

[trialAccount t18430821-2534]
2534. ELLEN COTTON was indicted for stealing, on the 18th of Aug., 2 pence and 7 halfpence; the monies of George Holborow, her master.
GEORGE HOLBOROW . I live in Clare-street, Clare-market—the prisoner was in my service. On the morning of the 18th of Aug., at six o'clock I watched her going to the water-closet—I had counted my money the previous night, and left 4 s. in copper in the till—part of it was marked—I sent for an officer, and she was searched, but nothing found on her—we found, hidden behind a tub in the water-closet, two pence and seven halfpence, which were marked.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. How long had she been in your service? A. About two months—I marked this money myself on the previous evening, at eleven o'clock—I have two young men, but they were in bed at the time—I had been to the water-closet myself before I called a policeman—I looked for it then, but could not find it.
JAMES HART . I live servant two doors from the prosecutor. The prisoner was constantly bringing in coppers to me to change for silver—I thought it was not right, and told the prosecutor.
LEE VINER ( police-constable F 143.) I found two pence and seven half-pence in the water-closet marked.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18430821-2535]
2535. ELLEN COTTON was again indicted for stealing on the 1st of Aug., 3 handkerchiefs, value 3 s.; 1 petticoat, 1 s.; 1 yard of silk, 3 s.; 1/2 yard of net, 3 s.; 1 cap, 5 s.; 2 pairs of gloves, 1 s.; and 1 gown, 3 s.; the goods of Martha Jane Taylor.
MARTHA JANE TAYLOR . I am niece to George Holborow. I searched the prisoner's box—no one was with me—I found three handkerchiefs, a petticoat, and some other things—they were mine—I had not given them to her or authorised her to take them.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Are you single? A. Yes—this property was kept in various places—a room in the attic was occupied by the prisoner, the floor above mine—there were a set of drawers in her bed-room—they were used by her—the bottom drawers had some of our bed furniture in it—I told her she might have the use of the middle drawer—this petticoat is mine—it has no mark on it—I have worn it—one of these handkerchiefs is marked with ink, and my night cap is marked with three letters—the prisoner was taken between six and seven o'clock—I went to her box between ten and eleven.
COURT. Q. Who had access to her room? A. No one that I know of—the two boys never went.
LEE VINER . I searched the box for the coppers, and found this purse in it; and this pincushion in a locked box, which the prisoner gave me the key of.
GUILTY . Aged 27.— Confined Three Months.
Fifth Jury, before Edward Bullock, Esq.
[trialAccount t18430821-2536]
2536. RICHARD VINCER was indicted for embezzlement.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
JAMES BLYTHE BRUNTON . I live, in Cheyne-walk, Chelsea, and am a baker. The prisoner was in my service for three weeks—it was his duty to receive money and to account for it either to me, or my daughter, or to the shopwoman—he did not pay me 2 l. 0 s. 9 d. from Mr. Tierney for bread up to the 5th of Aug.
COMFORT TIERNEY . I am the wife of Charles Tierney—we live in Paradise-row, Chelsea. I had a weekly account with the prosecutor—in this book
the amount for the week ending on the 5th of Aug. is 2 l. 0 s. 9 d.—the prisoner had latterly been in the habit of bringing the bread, and I paid him that week's account, 2 l. 0 s. 9 d.—it was my usual practice to hand orer this book to him to get Mr. Brunton's receipt to it, but on that occasion I forgot it—the following week, up to the 12th of Aug., the bread amounted to 2 l., 0 s. 9 1/2 d.—I paid him that on the 12th, and I said, "Why did not you ask me for the book last week to get it receipted, you know I paid you the money?"—he said, "I know you did."
Prisoner. I did not have the money that time. Witness. Yes, I know I paid it you.
ANN GAMMON . I am shopwoman to the prosecutor. The prisoner did not account to me for this 2 l. 0 s. 9 d. as which was owing on the 5th of Aug.
EUPHEMIA BRUNTON . I am the prosecutor's daughter. On the 12th of Aug. the prisoner brought me 2 l. 0 s. 9 1/2 d. as the amount of Mrs. Tierney's bread—he had handed me this book for the purpose of receipting it—I put my name to the bottom of it, which is the week ending on the 12th of Aug.—I then looked, and saw that the amount of the preceding week, the 5th of Aug., had not been receipted—I rubbed my name out from the bottom, and put it to the preceding week—he has not paid me the amount of the preceding week.
RICHARD BEARD ( police-sergeant V 17.) I took the prisoner—he said he had never received the money, or if he had he had paid it to the shop-woman.
Prisoner's Defence. I did not receive it.
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined Four Months.
[trialAccount t18430821-2537]
2537. THOMAS BENNETT was indicted for embezzlement.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
THOMAS BROWN . I am an earthenware and glass-dealer, in Rosemary-lane and at Walworth. The prisoner was in my service for four years and a half—it was his duty to account to me for the money he received immediately he received it, if I was at home, if not to my son, or a young man who had the management of the books—on the 6th of June he did not account to me for 5 s. received from Westbrook—I cannot call to mind whether I was at home on that day, but I am always there at seven o'clock in the morning, and am generally the last there at night—on the 11th he did not account for 2 s. 6 d. received from Dowling—on the 21st he did not pay me 10 s. from Smith—I recollect that day, and recollect his being on my premises after he had been his journey.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Will you swear you were not out all day? A. No—I was there in the morning, and in the evening—he had a private book of his own in which he used to make entries—he made no entry in my books—he called his account from his own book, and the clerk generally made the entry in my book—my books are not here—he has come in sometimes and laid down the money, and been told to go into the cellar to get something.
MR. DOANE. Q. What was the reason for his going into the cellar? A. If he was accounting to me for money—a person came and wanted a brown pan—I should say, "Go and get one," but that would not interrupt his accounting—he would go on with his accounts afterwards—I will swear he did not pay me this money on the 21st of June.
WILLIAM BROWN . I am the prosecutor's son. On the 6th of June, the prisoner did not pay me 5 s. from Westbrook—nor 2 s. 6 d. on the 11th from
Dowling—nor 10 s. on the 21st, as received from Smith—he had paid me 10 s. as received from Smith at different times—he did not pay me this.
Cross-examined. Q. Can you recollect particular days? A. No—I do not keep the books—if he pays me money, I hand it to my father with a list.
HENRY CHRISTIE . I am the prosecutor's clerk—the prisoner did not account to me for either of these three sums.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you keep the books? A. Yes—I enter the sums which I receive.
SARAH WESTBROOK . I am a widow, and live in Providence-row, Homerton. I know the prisoner as bringing goods from Brown—on the 6th of June, I paid him 5 s. on account of his master—this is his receipt.
SUSAN DOWLING . I am the wife of John Dowling, who keeps a china shop in Hackney-road. I paid the prisoner 2 s. 6 d., on the 11th of June for his master.
SUSAN SMITH . My husband lives at Newmarket-terrace, Cambridge-heath. I paid the prisoner 10 s. on the 21st of June, on account of his master.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18430821-2538]
2538. THOMAS BENNETT was again indicted for stealing, on the 17th of Aug., 18 dram glasses, value 12 s. 6 d.; the goods of Thomas Brown his master.
THOMAS BROWN . I deal in glass, and carry on business in Rosemary-lane. The prisoner was my journeyman—the glasses which are the subject of this charge were kept up one pair of stairs in the glass-room—he would have ready, access to that room I put great confidence in him—on the 17th the had to go out with some pattern glasses—I ordered him to get his patterns ready, which he did while I was at breakfast—when I returned he had got his basket ready—I told him to wait a minute, and to get two old patterns of blue cups and saucers to take to another person—while he was doing that I put my finger into his basket, and found there was something—I said, "Never mind that, we shall have some more"—I went to the end of the shop, and called out, "How many patterns have you got?"—he said twenty-nine—he then left, and I followed him—when I got to North East-passage, Welclose-square, I saw a policeman—I desired him to go and stop the prisoner—he stopped and asked what he had got in his basket—he said, "Nothing but some patterns"—I felt in his basket, and said, "You may take him to the station"—when we got there I examined the basket, and found twenty-nine patterns, and these eighteen publicans' dram glasses—he said it was a trick, that any of the men in the house would hang him, and some of them had put them there—these are the glasses—there are three different sizes, six of each—they were at the bottom of his basket—he could not have put the twentynine patterns in without knowing these were there.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Would they not all lie in the bottom of the basket? A. Yes—I have seven or eight persons in my employ—three of them are men—I am not aware that there had been any quarrel between the prisoner and the other men—he is a bad tempered fellow, and the men could not agree with him occasionally—I do not think any of them would hurt him.
JOHN WILSON ( police-sergeant H 13.) The prisoner was given to me by the prosecutor. I asked what he had got—he said, "Twenty-nine pattern glasses"—I took him to the station, and found the pattern glasses and these other glasses—he said it was a trick, and the carman wou'd hang him.
THOMAS BROWN re-examined. Q. Was the carman near the basket when the prisoner packed it? A. No, he was in the stable.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 19.— Confined Nine Months.

2545. ARTHUR LLOYD was indicted for obtaining goods by false pretences.
JOHN HANTON . I am a bookseller, and live in Museum-street. On a Monday, about three weeks ago, the prisoner came to my shop—I knew him before—he had lived with Alexander M'Kenzie, Esq., who dealt with me—he said he came from Alexander M'Kenzie for some books—he mentioned some books, and looked out others—he had before had books, as coming from Mr. M'Kenzie; in general, Mr. M'Kenzie used to send a note, but, knowing the party, I did not hesitate at that time—I delivered them to the prisoner—there were seven of them, and they were worth 2 l. 1 s.—I let him have them, supposing he came from Mr. M'Kenzie—two or three days afterwards I saw him in the street—he said he might as well give himself up—he then ran away, and I did not see an officer near me.
ALEXANDER M'KENIE . I live in George-street, New-road. The prisoner was in my service up to the 27th of July—I did not send him, on a Monday, about three weeks ago, for any books to Mr. Hanton—he did not bring me any—Mr. Hanton afterwards brought me the account, which I would not pay.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 23.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Two Months.
