ESSEX CASES.
Before Mr. Baron Rolfe.
[trialAccount t18430821-2548]
2548. GEORGE BARKER, CHARLES BROCKWELL , and JOSEPH WILKS were indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Sarah Thornhill, about two o'clock in the night of the 24th of June, at West Ham, with intent to steal, and stealing therein, 24 bottles, value 7 s.; 4 gallons of brandy, 6 l. 10 s.; 4 gallons of gin, 2 l. 10 s.; and 18 quarts of wine, 1 l. 15 s.; her goods.
MESSRS. BALLANTINE and HUDDLESTON conducted the Prosecution.
GEORGE VOOGHT . I live with my sister, Mrs. Sarah Thornhill, in the parish of West Ham, Essex. There is an orchard at the side of the house, between it and a row of houses, called the Printers'-buildings—the nearest house of that row is about a hundred yards off—they face West Ham-lane—there is a passage down the side, along the back, and up the middle of the houses, with a wall about eight feet high—on Saturday night, the 24th of June, I went to bed about half-past ten o'clock—the house was then safe—I was awoke by the policeman, about twenty minutes or half-past two—I found the back door open, and a hole bored into it, about the size of a quil—the door was not forced open—it was opened from the inside—some tiles were taken off the roof of the back kitchen, and a hole cut through the ceiling, by which a person could get through—the back kitchen communicates with the cellar, and we had lately had some bottles of Marsala, and spirits in stone jars placed in the cellar—when I came down in the morning I went with the policeman into the orchard—it was just getting light—we saw some tracks, and followed them—they led to a corner of the wall opposite the Printers', buildings, and by the side of that wall in the orchard I found about three dozen of wine in bottles, a loin of veal, some gooseberry-pies, and the principal contents of the larder—on the wall were some bottles of wine in a basket of ours, which had been in the back kitchen—we lost the tracks at that place—I afterwards examined the cellar, and missed from it four gallons of brandy, and four gallons of gin, in four stone bottles, and about a dozen and a half of wine besides the quantity we recovered in the orchard.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. How many houses are there in the Printers'-buildings? A. Thirty-two, I believe—No. 1 is the nearest house to mine—my sister is not in any business.
HANNAH CARR . I am single, and live at No. 3, Printers'-buildings, with Mrs. Craske, my aunt. It is the last house but two before you get to the orchard surrounding Mr. Vooght's house—Mr. Craske was out on Saturday night, and did not come home till between one and two o'clock on the Sunday morning—about that time my aunt told me something—I went to the back window, and saw a person on the wall surrounding Mr. Vooght's house—he remained there a great while, it seemed to me—it was not half an hour, but I was so frightened at the time, I cannot recollect—he was sitting right on the wall, and I could see his arm keep going over the wall backwards and forwards, as if he was taking something over—I believe that man was Wilks—I had seen him once or twice before as he passed the door—I do not know where he lived—he got down in front of the wall—his face was then turned sideways to me—I have
no doubt it was Wilks—at the time he was on the wall, I saw two men going from the corner of the wall, backwards and forwards, towards Ham-lane, at the back of Printers'-buildings, carrying something in front of them—I do not know what it was—they passed our house, and went down the road at the back—I know Both well and Barker live in the second row, but I do not know which houses—it is the farthest from the orchard, past the separation—that was the direction in which the two men were going.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. That it, they went beyond Nos. 2 and 1? A. Yes—whether they went to any of the houses I cannot tell—I do not know how many houses there are altogether—there is a passage up the middle of the houses—I did not see them so as to know whether they went beyond No. 14 or 15, or not—it is a common road.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Were any of your family taken up on this charge? A. Yes, my uncle was taken up the same Sunday—he came home between one and two o'clock in the morning—I and my aunt were at the front window, and we told him there were thieves at the back of the house—I went to the police-office on the Monday after my uncle was taken—I did not see him there—he was taken, I suppose, about two o'clock in the morning—I saw Wilks afterwards, between eight and nine in the morning—I did not converse with him—I heard my aunt do so—I had mentioned to my aunt and father that Wilks was the man I saw—I did not say so to Wilks himself—I did not know what to do—my aunt is not here—I did not mention to the police that Wilks was one of the men—I do not know that Wilks was taken before the Magistrate on the Monday, and discharged, there being no evidence against him—he was discharged on bail on the Wednesday after I gave evidence—I was not before the Magistrate while my uncle was in prison—I did not go before the Magistrate on the Monday—I waited outside—I was not in the room—I do not know a person named Stewart—I know a chandler's shop—I do not know the name of the person that keeps it—I did not give any account of this circumstance to that person—I did not name to a policeman that Wilks was one of the men till my uncle came out of prison, and that same evening I informed Waller the policeman—my uncle was discharged on the Monday—I did not say a word to the officer about Wilks till the Wednesday.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. How high are these houses? A. There is only one pair of stairs to go up—the wall at the back if higher than a man's head—there is a passage at the back of the houses—there if a paling to the gardens, but no wall.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. When did you see your uncle after he had been released? A. On the Monday morning—he came home then, and I mentioned to him what I had seen of Wilks—it was after that that I mentioned it to the policeman.
JAMES CRASKE . I am the uncle of the last witness, and live at No. 3, Printers'-buildings. On Saturday night the 23rd of June, I was out with a person named Mascall—we came home together on Sunday morning—we got to Printers'-buildings about a quarter or twenty minutes after two o'clock—my wife and niece were sitting up for me—when I got near No. 3, my wife threw the window up and told me something, in consequence of which I went to a policeman named Forrester, and received instructions from him, in consequence of which I went up the row as far as the passage, and as far as my house, No. 3—he told me to go in front and he would go to the back of the houses—I did so—Forrester went at the back of the houses between the two rows, between Nos. 14 and 15—when he went down the passage I saw Wilks come over the orchard wall close by the passage—I was then
standing at my own door—we were three cottages off—I ran after him, and told him to stop—he did so, and when I got up he turned and said, "You b—y fool, let me go"—I was so struck, seeing him and knowing him so well that I made a dead stop, and he turned round and ran off—Mascall was standing at my door talking to my wife and me at the time—I believe Wilks had no shoes on—they were very slight ones if he had, for I saw hit white stockings—I returned back, and Mascall asked me who it was—at that time Forrester came up from the middle gate—he turned back again, and Forrester took him into custody.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. There has been another burglary committed there since the prisoners have been in custody, has there not? A. Yes, at Mr. Holbrook's—I do not know how long ago.
cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. You were taken up yourself? A. Yes—Wilks's expression was not, "You b—y fool, let me go; don't you see it is me?"—it was, "You b-—y fool, let me go"—one of the other prsioner is related to me—Forrester asked me why I did not stop him—I do not recollect the words—it was to the effect that I let him go on purpose—I was taken about a quarter of an hour afterwards—I was before Mr. Streatfield, the Magistrate, on Monday—it was not the same Magistrate that Wilks was before—I remember his being discharged—I did not say a word to the Magistrate about Wilks being one of them—I wanted to speak, and he told me to hold my tongue—I did not tell the police—I told my own friends.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. What was the reason you did not tell the Magistrate? A. I was locked up—I said I wanted to speak to him, he said I had better not say any thing—I began three or four times, and he warned me not to say any thing—he said the policeman had given me in charge, and I was implicated with them for not stopping him—that was the reason I did not mention Wilks's name—at the time I did mention about him I was free—I first mentioned it to Waller the policeman—he came to my house—I then told him the story as I have told it now.
SAMUEL MASCALL . On Saturday evening, the 24th of June, I was in company with Craske—I returned with him to his house, from a quarter to half-past two o'clock—I saw his wife and Hannah Carr at the front window—they called to us—before that, Craske had made a communication to a policeman—I stopped at his door—he came back to me, after speaking to the policeman—I continued at his door—he stopped at it till I saw a young man come over the wall, and then he ran—I believe it was the prisoner Wilks—I asked Craske if it was not J. Wilks—I had every reason to believe it was—I heard some words pass between him and Craske—I cannot say what, as I was not near enough—after that he ran towards the Adam and Eve, away from Printers'-buildings—Wilks lives in the Cholera-houses; that is on the other side of the way from Printers'-houses—I know Barker and Brock well—I cannot say which houses they occupy.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. You were locked up on this case? A. Yes, from Sunday morning to Monday morning—the prisoner said something to Craske like this, "You b—y fool, let me go; don't you see it is me?"—I was at the door then, and saw Craske run to the policeman—I had been at Twig-folly that night—I do not know what it is more than other places—I was never there but once—a quantity of us went; perhaps nine—it is a street—we were in a public house before, and then we went to a private house to supper—I cannot say the name of the man who keeps the house—after the man ran away Craske came back to me—I did not say before the Magistrate that Wilks had any thing to do with it.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Did you hear the words, "Don't you see it is me"
or not? A. No—I did not say any thing to the Magistrate about Wilks—I first mentioned it in the week—I was not quite certain it was him.
THOMAS FORRESTER ( police-constable K 229.) On Sunday morning, the 25th of June, I was on duty at the Abbey-walk, West Ham-lane, near Printers'-buildings—I then saw Craske and Mascall—in consequence of information I received from them I went round to the back of Printers'-buildings through the passage that divides them—when I got to the back—I saw a man run through the passage opposite to me, in the direction where I had left Craske and Mascall—I went back and found them where I left them—I then sprang my rattle, and Mr. Tucker's watchman came up—I gave Craske and Mascall into his custody—I then went to Mr. Vooght's premises—I sprang my rattle—Mr. Vooght came down—I examined the cellar-door and the tiles, and then went with him to the wall—I there found some bottles of wine in a basket—I then returned and searched the cellar—I afterwards searched the orchard, and between the corner of the wall and the back of Mrs. Thornbill's premises there is a boarded fence—I saw a broken bottle on the grass near a hole in the fence which runs across the garden—I also found this centre-bit near to Mr. Vooght's back door, which was open.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did the man you saw run in the direction towards Printers'-buildings? A. Yes, in the direction of Mascall and Craske.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. How far does Mr. Holbrook live from this place? A. I do not know—I have heard of a burglary committed since these men have been in jail—Holbrook is one of the printers, I believe.
WILLIAM MARDELL . I am a labourer at Mr. Wagstaff's. I know the Cholera-houses—I do not know whether Wilks lives there—I found a bottle in a ditch opposite the Cholera-bouses—there are only two houses—I tasted it, but do not know what was in it—it was a stone-bottle—it made me rather giddy—I put it into the stable of Mr. Wagstaff's yard—the bottle is here now.
BENJAMIN HOLLOWAY ( a prisoner.) I am now in Newgate on a charge of stealing two duplicates—I know Brockwell and Barker by sight—I went of my own accord to see Barker's sister at a lodging-house in Keate's-court, Keste-street, Spitalfields—I do not know the day of the month—I did not then know of this robbery—I saw his sister—she told me her two brothers were taken—it was on a Tuesday, about eight weeks ago, as near as I can recollect—I first saw Barker about five o'clock the same evening to Kente's-court, with Brockwell—I went there the next day—I found them there then—they were out when I first went—I went to see his sister, as I was a friend of hers—I was there about an hour and a half—they were out at the time—I went, altogether, three or four times to Keate's-court, and saw Barker and Brockwell each time—they asked me if I would go down to their houses for some bottles which contained some ketchup, and whether I would bring them away—I said, Yes—both of them asked me to do it—they did not tell me where I should find the bottles—they said a person down there would give them to me when I got there—they said they were in the habit of making a lot of ketchup—I did not know their houses-Barker said his home was No. 37, Tucker's-buildings—I do not know where Brockwell's house is—I did not go down about the bottles—while I was there a person came in and said there was a robbery at Plaistow on the Sunday—we laid, "Was there indeed?"—Brockwell said, "We knowwho it is"—I do not know that person—a good many persons lodged at that house—the person said a good many people were taken, and Barker said, "Perhaps, if I had been down there, I should have been taken innocently"—Brockwell said the same, and said he should not like to be dragged through the streets for a thing that he knew nothing
about—some man said there were some wines, and brandies, and things left down there—I never heard them say anything about that—they had mentioned to me about the bottles of ketchup on the Monday-—the person that came on the Sunday, said there were some bottles found on Tuesday, in the privy at the back of the houses—I did not hear which—Barker said, "I dare say my wife has been foolish enough to throw down the bottles that I had, thinking they would correspond with the bottles lost, as the person is taken that I know"—both of them said that—Barker said he had got some bottles by him that he had from his father—I did not hear whether one or both spoke—they were both talking at the time—there were half a dozen persons in the room—Barker and Brock well were talking—they were the only two persons that made any answer about the bottles—nothing was said about a stone bottle in my presence.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. This conversation was in the room where a number of persons were, indiscriminately? A. Yes, a lodging-house—it was said loud enough for anybody to hear it—everybody appeared as much interested as these men—I never heard them say anything about it—Barker said he siad two or three bottles of ketchup at home that was getting musty, and would I go down and fetch it—it was the only message I had about the bottles—he said if I went down I might have them, he had made them of a good deal of champilions.—I never was in trouble before.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. When did you first tell anybody about this conversation? A. Six weeks ago, I think—there were eight or ten men and women in the room, coming up and down—I am waiting my trial in the other Court.
HUGH M'GREGOR . I am police-sergeant at Plaistow-station. On the 29th of June, in consequence of information, I searched Barker's house—I found nothing—I afterwards went into the privy—we had the place opened, and emptied, and in the dirt found eleven bottles, all broken but three—I have the bottoms and tops—in the necks of three of these bottles I found corks, which I have here—there was some wire on the top of two of the bottles—I searched Brock well's house, which is next door but one to Barker's—(Barker's is 25, Brockwell's, 27,)—I searched the privy in the same manner as Barker's, and found the paunches of two lambs, three bottles, and the neck of a fourth—there were no corks in those—they were all broken—I took Barker and Brockwell into custody on the morning of the 6th of July, in a house in Keate's-court, Thrawl-street, Whitechapel—I had been searching for them before, but could not see them till then—they were in bed together—I searched the bed, and found a stock, three centre-bits, and a small chisel, between the bed and the sacking—Barker and Brockwell used to work at Mr. Tucker's—I had been there and inquired, and could not find them there—in taking them to the station, handcuffed together, Barker said, "You would not have found us if you had not been told"—he also said, he supposed we had taken them for a robbery on account of their running away, and he said, Mr. May, my mate, seemed very merry at a poor fellow being transported—May was with me—these are the three bottles I found—I also produce a stone jar, which I got from Mardell—I also produce some pads which I saw May take out of a pair of stockings in Keate's-ceurt—they are used by housebreakers to walk lightly on the floor.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDEROAST. Q. Did you find these things at Brockwell'? A. Yes—I was standing there while the men were emptying the privy, and helped to take them out—this is all the glass that was found there—the privy is in the yard adjoining the house—the door of the wash-house and door of the privy are about a yard separate—the yard is only separated
from the others by a small paling—each yard is separated from the others, and from the lane, by a paling—the homes have very lately been built—I never observed that the paling was broken down—it is new—I was leaning against it all the time this was emptied—it is between four and five feet high, I think—it is an open fence, a little width apart—a girl came and opened the door to me at Keate's-court—there was another girl sitting on the hed—there were four people in the room—I was never there before—I have heard it is a common lodging-house—I have seen Brockwell more than once coiming out of the house that I searched—I am in the habit of being on duty there night and day, and passing the house—I will not swear to more than once, but I am satisfied I have seen him more.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. How do you know the other house you went to was Barker's house? A. Barker was not there at the time, but I saw his wife there—she told me she was his wife.
JOHN TUCKER . I own Printers'-buildings—it is also called Tucker's-buildings—Barker lived at No. 27, at the latter end of June, and Brockwell lived at No. 28.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Do you know Wilks? A. He was in my employ—he was taken from his business to be apprehended on this charge—he had been in my service ten years and a half—I can say nothing against his character.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. How long has Barker been in your employ? A. About the same time—they both served their apprenticeship—I kept Barker for about eighteen months afterwards—he was at work for me in the latter end of June—he and Brockwell worked two days after the robbery, and then absconded.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. What days were they? A. Monday and Tuesday—they left work unfinished—they gave no notice of their intention to go.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDEROAST. Q. How long has Brockwell been with you? A. Four or five years, perhaps—I am a silk-printer, and have from 600 to 700 men in my employ.
JOHN ANDERSON . I am governor of Ilford jail. On Monday, the 10th of July, I was in the yard in which Barker was confined—I said, "So, I see, you are remanded again"—he said, "Yes, because they say we have sent a lad, or a boy, out of the way, and told him where some of the things were put; we did not say that we put them there ourselves, but that some one else might have put them there."
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Were you examined before the Magistrate? A. No, but I mentioned this to the Magistrate.
MR. VOOGHT re-examined. Q. Were these the sort of bottles in which your wine was? A. Yes—these two contained soar beer—there was a seal on this, with the word "marsala" on it—this looks like the seal on the bottles in my cellar—I cut this off one of the bottles found in the orchard—my beer-bottles were fastened like these—I have got two bottles similarly secured—this stone jar is a similar one to that which contained the spirits.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. I believe Wilks was discharged on bail before the Magistrate? A. Yes—my sister keeps the house, and the property is hers.
RALPH HINDS . I am servant to Charles Woolley, a wine-merchant, About the 15th of June I delivered some Marsala wine, some rum, and gin, at Mr. Vooght's house—the top of this cork is some of my work—it is my master's seal—the corks of the marsala were sealed with it, and all of this colour—this appears to be the same—this is a similar jar to one in which I delivered the spirits.
CHARLES MAY ( police-inspector.) I found this part of a poker in a corner of the room in the house in Keate's-court.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Recorder.

2551. WILLIAM MACEY was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of July, 3 1/4 lbs. of oxalic acid, value 4 s.; the goods of Henry Samuel Crane and another, his masters.
JOHN ROYAL . I am foreman to Henry Samuel Crane and Son, Chemists, at Stratford—the prisoner was in their service. On the morning of the 12th of July I received information about oxalic acid—about six o'clock the bell rung, and the prisoner was going home—he was stopped at the counting-house door, and I asked if he had any objection to being searched, he said, "Not in the least"—I took him in the counting-house, and saw some oxalic acid in his hat and some in his pocket—I suppose about 3 1/2 lbs—we had
missed it several times, but there was nothing that made me know whose property that was—I could not pretend to Say that was our make.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. You could not tell that you had missed any? A. We missed some—we had perhaps two or three cwt., but you can tell if there is a handfull or two gone—the prisoner had been in the service for two or three years—Mr. Crame stopped the prisoner, and sent for me—he said to him, "William, have you any objection to be searched?"—he said, "No"—he did not say that he took it with intention of giving it in at the counting-house to my knowledge—he did not pull it out of his hat till he was in the counting-house—I believe he mentioned about taking a little for a bonnet—I should think all that he had about him he took out of his own accord—I believe there was nothing taken from him by anybody but himself—I was there at the time.
PETER M'QUILLAN ( police-constable K 375.) About six o'clock that evening the prisoner was given to me by Mr. Crane, with some oxalic acid.
HENRY CRANE . I am partner with my father, Henry Samuel Crane. The prisoner was going out—I was in the yard, and called him back—I believe all had gone out but the prisoner—he said he should like to have a proper person to search him—I said I was there, and the foreman, I thought that was sufficient, and then he submitted tolerably well—he took off his hat, and part of the oxalic acid was in it tied in his handkerchief—I said we would get a proper person to search him and take him, in the mean time the foreman got some more out of his pocket—I was at that time just outside the door—I have not a doubt that it was our property—we discovered that some had been taken by certain marks on a small parcel—I compared what was found on him with the bulk.
Cross-examined. Q. It is all alike, is it not-? A. Not always—I could swear to oxalic acid under some circumstances—this was rather a bad colour—it was not quite finished—when he took it from his hat he said he only took it for an old woman's bonnet—he said I should find nothing about him—he has worked with us several years—he has a wife.
NOT GUILTY .

2553. REBECCA AGGAR was indicted for stealing, on the 21st of Aug., 1 peck of wheat in the ear, value 9 d.; the property of George Biggs.

I live at Barnard Castle farm, Barking—on the 21st of August, I saw the prisoner near some shocks of corn—I rode round to her, and found her with a large quantity of ears of wheat in her apron, and a bag near her with ears of corn in that—they were the same as the ears that were in the field—I asked what she was doing—she said she thought she was doing no harm—the shocks of corn were in the field, and she had no business there.
Prisoner. I broke off the ears—I did not know the rules of the field—I never had been gleaning before in my life, and I am afraid they told me wrong—I neither took an ear of corn from the shocks, nor yet touched the shocks, only what was between the shocks.

MARY ANN SWEET . I am the prosecutor's daughter—I was with my mother, and saw the prisoner taking the things off the stall which she did not buy, and put them into her pocket.

2556. JOHN DONLEY was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of Aug., 2 table cloths, value 7 s.; the property of Hugh Manley Tuite.
PATRICK PALPY . I am servant to Capt. Hugh Manley Tuite, at the barracks, at Woolwich—on the 16th of Aug., I had three table cloths of his safe in No. 29 quarters—I missed them that afternoon, between ten and twelve minutes after seeing them—these now produced are them—I do not know the prisoner—I saw him that day about one hundred paces from ray master's quarters.
THOMAS ARMSTRONG . On the 16th of Aug., about nine o'clock in the evening, the prisoner came to my shop, and asked if it was a pawnbroker's—I
said, "No," there was one in Air-street, but they were shut up—he offered me these table-cloths for half-a-crown—I sent for a policeman, and gave him into custody—he told me his wife was very ill, and he wanted to make some money of them.
Prisoner's Defence. I was formerly in the dragoons, and went to the barracks frequently, to see some comrades. I was very kindly treated, both by officers and men; they subscribed for a suit of clothes for me, and told me on the day in question, that if I went to the tailor, I should get them, and also some money. I was about getting into the police again; and it is not likely I should take these things, after such kindness. As I left the barracks a woman met me, gave me the clothes, and told me to say I had them from my wife, and I was to pass the night with her—she was a stranger to me; she told me to go to this shop.
EDWARD PALMER ( police-constable R 231.) I heard one of the tailors say that he was very much surprised the prisoner should commit a felony, for they had collected for a suit of clothes foi him, for some religious institution, and likewise a little money, but they returned it, on hearing what he had done.
GUILTY . Aged 40.— Confined Four Months.

[trialAccount t18430821-2558]
2558. DANIEL SHEEN was indicted for feloniously assaulting Caroline Young, on the 13th of July, and cutting and wounding her on her head, with intent to do her grievous bodily harm.
CAROLINE YOUNG . I am the wife of James Young, and live in Old King-street, Deptford, the prisoner lodged with us. On the 30th of July I saw that he had a pair of my husband's shoes on, and asked how be came by them, he made no answer—I asked him again, he gave me no satisfaction—I said he owed me quite enough, without taking them to wear—he called me a wh—he was at work, making a backing-cap—he is a deal porter—he jumped up with a knife in his hand, and stabbed me over my left eye—it bled—I laid hold of the handle of the knife, and got it away from him—he knocked me about afterwards, but still I wish to recommend him to mercy, for the sake of his wife and children—he did not touch my throat—I pointed out the knife to the policeman when he came.
JOHN WHITE ( police-constable R 180.) I took the prisoner into custody, and told him I took him on a charge of stabbing a woman—he said, "I acknowledge to striking her, but not with the knife"—I went to his room, and
the prosecutrix pointed out the knife to me—her forehead was cut, and a great deal of blood was running from it—it was a clean cut wound, such as a knife would make.
EDWARD DOWNING . I examined the prosecutrix's temple—it had an incised wound, a little better than a quarter of an inch long—I cannot tell the depth—it was two days after—it was such a wound as this knife would make—the skin was divided—there was a plaster on it.
Prisoner's Defence. I was mending my own shoes, and her husband leat me his to put on; his wife said I had stolen them. I begged her to let me tell her how I came by them; she would not—seeing her in a passion, I picked up my knife and awl, and wax, intending to go up stairs out of her way; when she seized me by the hankerchief, and was in the act of strangling me. My wife ran down stairs, and I gave the knife and things to her. I begged of the prosecutrix to let go of my handkerchief, and she would not: her boy was behind, kicking me. I did not like to hit her, and pushed her against a barrow in the yard—I was obliged to strike her with my first twice in the eye before she would let me go.
CAROLINE YOUNG re-examined. I did not seize the prisoner by the handkerchief—he hit me after I got the knife away from him—he did not say anything when he struck me with the knife—he seemed in a passion—I was not advancing towards him—I rather drew back from him—I was standing before him, and he advanced towards me—he got up with the knife in his hand—I thought he was going up stairs—I did not get in his way—I do not know whether it was accidental—I was looking more at his feet than his hands—I cannot positively say that he did it wilfully.
EDWARD DOWNING re-examined. I do not think the wound could have been made by falling against a barrow, it would have been more jagged.
GUILTY of an Assault. Aged 28.— Confined Fourteen Days.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18430821-2559]

2561. HENRY LEADBURY was indicted for stealing, on the 21st of July, 1 pair of trowsers, value 4 s. 6 d.; 1 waistcoat, 1 s.; and 1 handkerchief, 6 d.; the goods of Joseph Maybank.
JOSEPH MAYBANK . I do not know the prisoner—I never sent him for my trowsers, waistcoat, and handkerchief.
SUSANNAH MAYBANK . I am the prosecutor's wife—we live in Creek-street, Deptford. On the 21st of July, the prisoner came and told me my husband had met with a misfortune, and had sent him for some clothes—I believed his story, and gave him a waistcoat, a pair of trowsers, and a handkerchief I am sure he is the man.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. You never saw the man before? A. No—it was on Friday he came to me, and on the Tuesday afterwards I saw him in the churchyard, and gave him into custody—he said some vitriol had been dropped on my husband's clothes—I was rather alarmed—he said my husband was not hurt—I have said that I thought the prisoner was the man.
COURT. Q. Are you sure he is the man? A. Yes—I think he is—he came to me on Friday the 21st of July, at twenty minutes or a quarter before twelve o'clock.
THOMAS BRYDGES . I live at No. 9, Creek-street, Deptford, four doors from Mrs. Maybank. On the 21st of July, a man who I think was the prisoner, came to my place, from a quarter past eleven to half-past—he asked if there was a man named Chuck, who drove a cart—I said there was an old man—he said he did not want the old man—he wanted the young one.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you hear that Mrs. Maybank had been imposed on by somebody? A. Not till I came home at night.
WILLIAM AYRES . On Friday, the 21st of July, I was at my master's yard in Cooper's-lane—two or three minutes' walk from Creek-street—about twelve o'clock the prisoner came and asked if a man worked there named Chuck—I said, "No"—he said he did not want the old man, he wanted the young man—he asked me if he was at home—I told him no—he asked his name and where he lived—I said his name was Maybank, and he lived the third house down Creek-street—I am sure the prisoner is the man.
Cross-examined. Q. That was the first time you saw him? A. Yet—I did not see him again until he was in custody—I heard, about two hours after I saw the man, that a trick had been played.
MR. HORRY called
MARIA ROBERTS . I live at No. 47, James-street, Camberwell New-road. My husband is a salesman—the prisoner and his wife lodge in my house—on Friday, the 21st of July, he was at home till twelve o'clock—I had known him for about a month previous—he was taken into custody on the Tuesday following.
COURT. Q. How do you come to recollect Friday the 21st? A. I have been in the habit of giving his wife washing, and I did so that day—some of the things wanted mending, and I wanted her to sit in my room and do them—she said she would rather not, for her old man was up stairs, and she wished him to go out, as she did not like to have washing about in the room when he was at home—I know this was on Friday as that is my washing day—she has always washed on Friday ever since she has been there—Mary
Berry was in the house at the time, and I believe she saw the prisoner—she is not here—I am not aware that any one else was present—the prisoner was up stairs in his room—I saw him when he came down to go out—he had light kerseymere trowsers and waistcoat on, and the jacket he has on now—I have not known Hempstead previously, but I have seen him here to day—he is a journeyman baker, and lives in Southampton-street, Camberwell—I did not come here with him—I came with the prisoner's wife—the prisoner came back between five and six, or six o'clock.
MR. HORRY. Q. Had the beer man come round? A. He was coming up the street—he comes at twelve o'clock, and the prisoner was then going out.
WILLIAM HEMPSTEAD . I am a journeyman baker, in the employ of Mr. Horsley, of Southampton-street, Camberwell. The prisoner is a journeyman baker also—I remember his being taken into custody on a Tuesday—on the Friday previous to that, I saw him in Camberwell New-road, as I was carrying my twelve o'clock bakings home—I had my board on my head—he remained with me till a quarter past one o'clock—he went along with me by my master's shop.
COURT. Q. How was he dressed? A. He had a pair of light coloured trowsers—I think they were pepper and salt colour.
WILLIAM AYRES re-examined. Q. What coloured trowsers had the man on that you saw? A. A pair of light kerseymere trowsers—I am sure it was about twelve o'clock—my place is four or five miles from Mrs. Roberts.
NOT GUILTY .

2563. JOHN WESTON and JOHN BROWN were indicted for stealing, on the 19th of Aug., 1 purse, value 1 d.; and 4 half-crowns and 1 sixpence; the property of Stuart M'Garry, from the person of Maria M'Garry.
MR. PRENDERGAST conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM GLADWIN ( police-constable R 122.) I was at Woolwich on Saturday night, the 19th of Aug., at a quarter or twenty minutes before ten o'clock. I saw the prisoners coming in a direction from Mr. Smith's shop, about twenty or thirty yards from it, and knowing one of them I took them to the station—I found two half-crowns, three shillings, and twopence on Weston, and two half-crown, five shillings, and threepence on Brown—they both said that they were hawkers, that they had no home, and had come from Tunbridge Wells races.
JAMES PARRY ( police-sergeant R 8.) The prisoners were brought to the station, and this money was delivered to me by Gladwin—the half-crown that is recognised by the prosecutor was found on Weston.
MARIA M'GARRY . I am the wife of Stuart M'Garry. On Saturday night, the 19th of Aug., I received seven half-crowns from my husband—I paid my rent with some of it—I then went to the butcher's to buy something—I went to pay for it, put my hand in my pocket, and my purse was gone—I had had at that time four half-crowns and one sixpence in my purse—the rest I had paid away—when my husband gave me the half-crowns, he chucked one of them on the table, and said, "There is one that is marked, I don't know whether it is good or not"—I said, "Master would not give you a bad one"—I took it up, but did not look at it—I put it in my purse—I do not know
on which side the mark was—I know I had my purse safe in my pocket when I got to the butcher's shop—I put my hand on my pocket as I was going up.
STUART M'GARRY . On Saturday night, the 19th of Aug., I gave my wife seven half-crowns, which I had taken from my employer—I looked at them all over before I gave them to her—I noticed one of them—this is it—(looting at one)—it has a small mark over the Victoria, and another mark on the other side, over the Britannia—I am sure I observed this at the time sufficiently to be able to swear to it—this is one of the two that was found on Weston.
cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. What are you? A. A tailor.
GEORGE HARRIS ( police-constable R 158.) I am stationed at Woolwich. I was at Mr. Smith's, the butcher's, that night—I saw Brown standing on the pavement, outside the shop—I heard Mrs. M'Garry say she had been robbed—Brown had looked at some meat, and asked the price of it—I did not see weston.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

2567. JOHN BASHFORD was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of July, 1 bag, 6 d.; 9 sovereigns, 5 half-sovereigns, and 3 5 l. promissory notes; the property of Henry Robert Coulter, in the dwelling-house of Mary Harris.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
HENRY ROBERT COULTER . I am a farmer—I live at Deddington, in Kent—I have used the Nag's Head, Borough, for thirty-eight years when I came to town—I left home on the 13th of July, and had five half-sovereigns and some silver in a bag—I went through Sittingbourne, and there received three 5 l. notes, nine sovereigns, a half-sovereign, and 6 d., t and arrived in town about eight o'clock in the evening—I supped with the landlady, and went to bed
about twelve—I was quite'sober—when I came in in the evening, I felt my money quite safe in my left hand pocket—I doubled my breeches up, and laid them under the bolster—I had buttoned my pocket—I awoke next morning about four o'clock, just looked out of the window, laid down again, and went to sleep—I awoke by hearing a noise like the jingling of money—I said nothing for a few seconds—at last, thinking I heard somebody on the left hand side of the bed, I called out, "Who is there?"—I put the curtain aside, and there stood "boots" (the prisoner) by the side of the bed—I said, "Halloo, are you come for my shoes?"—he said, "No, I came to call you," and that he was ordered to call me at five o'clock—I said, "I gave no orders"—I saw him distinctly, and think it impossible I can be mistaken—I knew his voice, and saw his side face—he walked out of the room, saying, "I have made a mistake then, for I had to call somebody"—being one of the servants I did not at first think anything of it, but in a few minutes I examined my trowsers, which I found just where I had put them—the pocket was not turned out, but unbuttoned and drawn up towards the top, and my bag and money were gone—I got up, went down stairs—I sat in the coffee-room, and gave him my shoes to bpush, which he did—he put them down, and turned to go out—I then asked him why he came to call me—he said he had not been in my room at all—I told him I had lost a parcel—he said he knew nothing of it—I then told him what it was—he said there was more in the house than him, there was sweeps sweeping the chimney, and people had got up and left about five o'clock, he did not know who they were, and there was another servant—I said, was he up?—he said "No"—he asked if he should call him—I said, "Do as you please"—he said, "It might be another person"—I said, "No, I am satisfied it is you"—I then considered I might as well see the other, though I was certain of him—I said I should go and stop payment of the notes—when I returned the waiter and prisoner were both there—when I saw the waiter I was positive it was not him—when I charged the prisoner with it about six o'clock he said, "Have you searched? you may have dropped it in the room; you was out in the evening, perhaps it might be picked out of your pocket"—I said, "No, I am quite certain it was safe when I went to bed"—the chambermaid afterwards produced my bag, containing three 5 l. notes—the gold was gone—I had been at the Nag's Head last June, but should not have known the prisoner if I had met him in the street before that, as a servant of the house—I am satisfied he is the man who was in my bed-room.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. When he said there was another servant, did you say you could tell better if you saw the servant? A. I said "If I saw you together, I should be more positive"—I should know whether it was the other or not—it was broad day light when he came into my room, and I had drawn the window curtain at four o'clock—the prisoner said it was not him—I said, "You had a short jacket when you was in the room"—he said he had had no other dress on that day—I said if he chose to put on a jacket and come into the room he might, but he did not—I said at six o'clock I should go and stop payment of the notes, but I did not, it was too early—I went to the barber's, and got shaved—I returned in about an hour—I did not tell the barber about this, nor meet a policeman—when I saw the other servant I said, "I am satisfied it was not you"—Mrs. Harris was ill—I did not like to disturb the house—about nine o'clock I said I must go out, I would leave it in their hands till I returned to take what steps were right—I had several things to do—I came back about three o'clock, and then they spoke to the policeman, and I gave him in charge—I found my breeches not moved from under the pillow, but heaved up so that they could get at the pocket—the pillow was more than two feet long,
and I was laying at the end of it—I got to the house about eight o'clock in the evening—I went out as far as St. George's church with a friend, and stood a minute talking by the railings—I know all was safe when I returned—the prisoner was admitted to bail.
ROBERT L. HARRIS . My mother keeps the Nag's Head. The prisoner has been about two years and a half in our establishment—he had notice to quit before this, and was to have left the following day.
Cross-examined. Q. Has not a gentleman, sleeping in this room since, complained of something? A. Not to me—we had three or four gentlemen sleeping in the room over this, and I heard that one of them went into this room by mistake—two carriers slept in the house on the night of the robbery—they regularly sleep there twice a week—I understand the chimneys were swept that morning.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did you take the prosecutor's money? A. Certainly not—nobody else has complained of losing money in the house—the prosecutor frequented our house.
CHARLOTTE FENNER . I am chambermaid at the house. I went into the prosecutor's room between seven and eight o'clock in the morning, as the prisoner told me there Was a mess in the corner of the room—I went up with Jewson, and found there had been a cat in the corner—we looked into the bed, and under the bolster Jewson found a bag, with three notes in it—I took them to Mr. Coulter—there was no gold in it—the bag could not have got by accident where I found it, for it was tucked under the bolster with the sheet.
Cross-examined. Q. Was it wrapped up in the sheet? A. No, tucked up with it—I have heard something of a complaint from a person sleeping in the room since—the prisoner told me Mr. Coulter told him there was a mess in the room, and that he had lost some notes.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Was there any complaint of any thing being lost from the room? A. Nothing of the kind.
SARAH JEWSON . I am chambermaid at the inn. I went into the room between seven and eight o'clock in the morning, and found the bag and notes under the bolster, tucked in with the bottom sheet.
cross-examined. Q. Did you hear a complaint made by a Mr. Minny sleeping in the room afterwards? A. He complained, three weeks after this, of somebody coming into the room, and he asked what they wanted—they said they mistook the room—I never found out who it was—the sweeps had been in the house on both occasions.
WILLIAM TILLIARD . I am waiter at the house. I got up on the morning in question about a quarter past eight o'clock.
THOMAS BENSON . I am a carrier, and put up at the Nagg's Head. I slept there on this night—I got up about five o'clock, and left at half-past eight—Constable. who is too ill to attend, slept in another part of the house.
Cross-examined. Q. You saw the prisoner that morning? A. Yes—he was cleaning his boots when I came down—the sweeps had gone before I came down.
JOHN YATES ( police-inspector.) I took the prisoner, searched him and his box, but found nothing—he said he had never been in Mr. Coulter's room that morning.
NOT GUILTY .

2568. WILLIAM SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of August, 1 coat, value 5 s., the goods of George Harris.
GEORGE HARRIS . I live at Tunbridge, in Kent. On the 15th of August I was at the Catherine Wheel, in the Borough—I had a coat there, which I
saw safe in the van in the yard, at a quarter to six o'clock in the morning, under the seat—I missed it at half-past six—this now produced is it.
WILLIAM BURFIN, JUN . I am "boots" at the Catherine Wheel. I saw the prisoner come away from the van, with the coat under his arm, that morning—I gave information to the constable.
WILLIAM BURFIN . I am a watchman. I was sent for, and spoke to the prisoner about the coat—he said he had not seen any coat—I said I insisted on his telling me where it was—he immediately said it was in the parlour—he went into the parlour, and pulled it from under the seat.
Prisoner's Defence. I intended going to Tunbridge Wells that day, and thought the van started at seven o'clock, instead of which, it was nine; I sat down in the parlour; two persons came in; I went to the water-closet at the end of the yard; there was a man there; I waited two or three minute, till he came out; I then went in, and found the coat there; I brought it out on my arm, carried it directly to the parlour, and chucked it on the seat; I then came and stood at the door till the watchman came and asked about it; I said, "I have got no coat, there is one on the seat;" if I intended to steal it I should have taken it away.
WILLIAM BURFIN, JUN . re-examined. When I first saw him he had the coat under his arm—I afterwards saw him with it on his back, underneath his own—I went to fetch a policeman—he was going from the van, towards the water-closet.
GUILTY . Aged 50.— Confined Four Months.
Before Mr. Justice Williams.

2569. ADAM ANDREWS, alias Osborne , was indicted for feloniously, and knowingly uttering a counterfeit half-crown; having been previously convicted of uttering counterfeit coin.
MESSRS. CHAMBERS and SCRIVEN conducted the Prosecution.
CALEB EDWARD POWELL . I am assistant solicitor to the Mint. I produce a copy of the record of the conviction of Adam Andrews, in Feb. last—I have examined it with the original in Mr. Clark's office—it is a true copy—( read.)
JOHN CLARK ( policeman.) I was present at the trial of Adam Andrews, in Feb. last—I am quite positive the prisoner is the man.
STEPHEN CONSTABLE . I am assistant to Mr. Stagg, linen-draper, of Newington-causeway. On the 28th of July, the prisoner came and asked for a cotton handkerchief—I said it came to 8 1/2 d.—he paid me a half-crown—I saw it was bad the moment he threw it on the counter, and showed Mr. Shield, our foreman, who took it, and sent for an officer—Lloyd came and took the prisoner and the half-crown away.
Prisoner. They had the half-crown for ten minutes, and might have mixed it with other money. Witness. I am quite certain it was not mixed with other money—when the prisoner said he did not know it was bad, he turned, round and walked about two yards towards the door—the foreman put this hand on his shoulders, and said, "You wait for a policeman."
ARCHIBALD SHIELD . I am foreman to Mr. Stagg. I received a half-crown from Constable, and gave it to Lloyd—the prisoner moved two or three yards to leave the shop—I got before him, and prevented him—I kept the half-crown in my hand, and did not mix it with other money.
JOHN LLOYD . I took the prisoner in charge at Mr. Stagg's, and produce the half-crown.
EMMA BROSTER . I am bar-maid at the New Inn, Westminster-road. On the 26th of July, at eight o'clock in the evening, the prisoner came to the bar for half a pint of beer, which came to 1 d.—he threw down half-a-crown—I
said it was bad—he said it was not, and ran away from the bar, leaving the beer untouched—I gave the half-crown to Mr. Williams, my employer, without mixing it with other money—on the Monday following I saw the prisoner in Horsemonger-lane, and picked him out from among at least twenty prisoners, without his being pointed out.
Prisoner. I never was in the house. Witness. I am certain of him—he had on a fustian dress, which he has repeatedly worn when he offered me money—I knew him well before—he gave me a bad shilling a fortnight before—I returned it, and he gave me a good one.
JOHN HENRY WILLIAMS . I keep the New Inn. On the 26th, my bar-maid gave me a bad half-crown—I called in Cotterell and gave it to him—we marked it—this is it.
WILLIAM ALLOTT . I am assistant to Mr. Lemon, cheesemonger, Walworth-road. On the 24th or 26th of July the prisoner came there for three halfpenny-worth of cheese, and gave me a good half-crown—I gave him 2 s. 4 1/2 d.—he then asked me to give him two sixpences for a shilling—I observed him place a bad shilling on the counter for me instead of one of those I gave him—I said, "If you will give me a shilling, I will give you two sixpences, but for that I cannot give them"—he said, "Why not?"—I said, "That is not a shilling"—he said it was one I had given him—I am positive it was not—I advised him to take his money, and go about his business, or I should cut it in two, and send for an officer—I allowed him to take it away after grumbling—I am sure it was a counterfeit shilling—I saw him at Union-hall the following Saturday—I was within half a yard of the shilling, and am certain it was bad.
MR. JOHN FIELD . I am inspector of counterfeit coin to Her Majesty's Mint. These two half-crowns are counterfeit in all respects.
GUILTY . Aged 33.— Transported for Ten Years.
Before Mr. Baron Rolfe.
[trialAccount t18430821-2570]
2570. GEORGE CLEMENTS was indicted for feloniously assaulting E. O. Buckley, and cutting and wounding him, with intent to do him grievous bodily harm.
E. O. BUCKLEY. I am a labourer, and live in Fawcett's-place, Bermondsey. The prisoner is a labourer, and lived about 600 yards from me—on the 1st of Aug. I was drinking with him from about ten till half-past one o'clock—I had a dispute with him, and we parted at that time—he went away—I remained—we were at a public-house in Tooley-street—I went to different houses, from the time he left me till eight o'clock, when I went home—I was very tipsy, and was led home—I do not know what happened—I was helpless—I did not see the prisoner in my way home—when I got home, I found I was wounded very badly indeed—I have been under the doctor's hands ever since, at the hospital.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Where were you first drinking with him? A. At the Dundee Arms, Bermondsey—the quarrel was not there—we went with a man to buy a pair of boots, and had a dispute about what leather it was that was at the shoemaker's shop in Tooley-street—we then went to a public-house—he threw a pot of porter in my face, and we had a few more words—we had seven or eight pots of beer among four of us, but no liquor—I did not threaten to punch his head—when he threw the porter in my face, I struck him, and knocked him down—he then left—I never said I got my wound by falling down or fighting with any man—I recollect breaking a pane of glass to get him out of a house, and I kicked the panel of the door in—my
coat was off when I cut the glass, and believe my shirt sleeves were tucked up—I was helpless drunk.
Q. Had you not been fighting a stand-up fight with him, and when the policeman came up, he ran away? A. Yes, he ran away when the policeman came—I tried to defend myself as well as I could—I was too drunk to stand—I will not swear I did not hit him—he knocked me down, and ill-used me, and I somehow got my coat off while some women held him—I broke three or four panes of glass with my fist, not with my arms—this was after he had runaway—it was before I knocked my head through the glass that I found myself at home, wounded—there was blood all over me before that—both my arms and head bled very much.
MARGARET BUCKLEY . I am fourteen years old, and am the prosecutor's sister. I met him in Russell-street, Bermondsey, between seven and eight o'clock—he was able to walk, but not well—he was very drunk indeed—I helped him home—I met him above a hundred yards from home—he went home quietly down the court—we met the prisoner in the court—he hit my brother, and knocked him down—the prisoner was tucking his shirt collar down when I met him—he struck my brother several times while he had him down, and every time he struck him blood flowed—I saw something glittering in his hand, like a knife—Robert Parker was sitting at the door of his sister's house—several persons came out of that house—Parker's brother came out, and pulled the prisoner from my brother—I saw my brother go into the door of his house, but I did go in.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you know of your brother being helped home by a policeman that night? A. No—he was drunk—his coat was on when I met him, but off when he came out of his own house again—it was on when the prisoner attacked him—he came out after I saw him home—there was no fighting then—the prisoner went into his motber-in-law's house, and when my brother came out, he broke some windows, and said Clements should come out and fight him again—I then saw a policeman, who took my brother home—there are scrapers outside my brother's house—he did not fall on the scraper.
COURT. Q. When your brother got home the first time, was he bleeding? A. Very much, from both arms and his head.
WILLIAM FENSON ( policeman.) I was called to Fawcet's-place on the 1st of August—I saw the prosecutor standing there, surrounded by several persons, bleeding from the head—I asked how he received that wound—he said he got it in falling with the prisoner, who was there, and walked away on my coming up—I took the prosecutor home—he had no wound on his arm, for I held him by the arm—it was about half-past eight o'clock—he was very drunk, more like a madman—he had no shirt on, nor anything but his trowsers—I went to the top of the court—I was called back again, and saw him fighting through the window at the prisoner, who was inside the house—he broke three panes in the window, and two over the door, and split the pannel of the door, trying to get in—he put his arms through the glass, and broke the door with his fist—I observed he was bleeding from the arms—he went in-doors to his wife—when the prisoner found he was gone in-doors, he came out and went away.
Cross-examined. Q. What condition was the prisoner in? A. Stipped, all but his shirt—the prosecutor's arms went right through the glass.
ROBERT PARKER . On the 1st of August, about eight o'clock, I was sitting at my sister's door, in Fawcet's-place, and saw the prisoner, who had been waiting two or three hours a few doors below my sister's, near the prosecutor's house—I had been in and out, and saw him there for two or three hours,
sitting out in the court, not doing anything, not drinking—Buckley-came home very tipsy—he came straight to my door, then the prisoner pitched into him—they both had their clothes on then, and both fell together—the prisoner did not seem tipsy—I did not see Buckley fall on anything but the stone paving—I saw the blood spring out after he fell two or three times, first from his eye, then from his head—there was some on his arm—I could not tell where it came from—my sister fetched Fenson, the policeman, who took him home, and went to the top of the court, when the prosecutor came out again, bleeding—he had bis clothes on then.
Cross-examined. Q. Had he got up each time he was knocked down? A. My brother-in-law took him up two or three times—he pulled off bis clothes after the first fall, and then they stood up to each other, and had a stand-up fight—I cannot say whether the prisoner fell himself, or was knocked down; they fell together, the prisoner undermost sometimes—I did not see the prisoner take off his clothes—he was without his coat—I was not the second of either of them.
BENJAMIN SAMUEL OPAL . I am assistant to a surgeon—I examined the prosecutor on the 1st of Aug., and found five wounds in his arm, and one on hit head—the wounds on the arm appeared to have been done by glass, not with a knife—that on the head was three or four inches long—the bone was completely denuded of its corering—I should say it was not done with a first—it might be done by falling on any thing sharp.
Cross-examined. Q. Could a fall on a scraper have done it? A. Certainly.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Justice Williams.
[trialAccount t18430821-2571]
2571. JAMES TRENATE, JAMES HUGGETT , and WILLIAM JONES were indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Alphonso Macintosh, on the 13th of August, at Lambeth, and stealing therein, 4 spoons, value 1 l. 10 s.; 1 pair of sugar tongs, 1 s.; 4lbs. weight of cigars, 5 l. 4 s.; 4 sixpences; 60 pence; and 96 half-pence; his property; and feloniously beating and striking the said Alphonso Macintosh, in the said dwelling-house.
MR. CROUCH conducted the Prosecution.
ALPHONSO MACKINTOSH . I keep the Old Red Lion, in Bridgt-stwet, in the parish of St. Mary, Lambeth—on the rooming of the 4th of Aug., I went to bed about two o'clock—I examined the house previous to going to bed—there is a door leading to the stable—I fastened that myself by two bolts, and shut down all the first floor windows—after retiring to bed, I was alarmed by hearing a policeman call me by name, and ringing the bell—I opened the window, and after hearing what he said, I went up stairs, called my waiter, and immediately came down stairs—I beard somebody, as I thought, trying to open the front door which faces the water—there are two fronts to my house, the back-door leads to the stable—the stable yard is generally open all night—when I got down stairs it was quite dark, in consequence of the house being cloied—I went towards where I heard the noise, and seized hold of somebody by the collar—I could not recognise him at that time—the man I seized, said, "You have been robbed, Sir, I am looking for the thief"—I said, "If that is the case, no harm can come to you;" at the same moment, I received a violent blow at the back of the shoulder—I could not tell what it was with—I then seized the man tight by the throat—I could not tell who he was at that moment—I then received another blow, on the temple, at the side of my eye—it did not quite stun me, but made me feel rather stupid—I
then caught him tighter, and put my legs round him, and flung him on the top of the kitchen stairs, on the landing, level with the ground floor—I threw myself on him, and held him till assistance came—the policeman B 53 came from the stable yard with others—somebody brought a light which enabled me to see the man—it was the prisoner, Huggett—I was upon him—I did not loose my hold of him at all—I am quite positive he is the person I first seized, and am sure he is the man who struck the second blow; because, while I held him, I felt his arm come against me—I saw nobody else when the light came—I then went into the bar, and found it strewed with various things—some silver spoons and forks were bent, and thrown about the bar—I found the bar door had been opened which I had locked at night, and taken the key to bed with me—I found the bar window had been forced by a crow-bar—I found one of the first floor windows open, which I am positive was shut when I went to bed—it was merely kept down by its own weight—I found the dust on the stone of the balcony scraped away by fingermarks, and the marks of a shoe with a nail—the balcony is about nine feet from the ground—a person could easily get up there, and from there get to the window—from a small cupboard in the bar, I missed three silver spoons, and a five shilling paper of copper—my bar-maid's work-box was forced open—the tills were emptied of four sixpences, and about four shillings of copper money—nearly five pounds weight of cigars were taken from inside the bar—I found many of them strewed about the passage leading to the stable yard, and one bundle, unbroken, in the stable yard—two stairs below where I struggled with Huggett, I picked up a life-preserver; and the 5 s. packet of halfpence I picked up where I threw Huggett—the life-preserver was close to the spot—about ten minutes after I went down stairs into the cellar, with the policemen, and heard a policeman say, "Here is another man"—he was in the cellar—we found the prisoner Jones in the coal cellar—after he was taken, we were searching among the coals, and on turning them over, found a crow-bar, which I afterwards fitted to the bar window, and it answered the marks exactly—I do not think any of the prisoners were in the house the previous night—the back-door was fastened before I went to bed.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Was it open when you got up? A. It must have been, because assistance came to me—I did not go to it—the first time I went to it, I found it open; that was when I gave Huggett in custody, which was five minutes after I came down stairs—that door had been bolted—I did not bolt it myself—I looked at it before I went to bed, and found it bolted—it was about two o'clock in the morning—I am sure I was the last person up in the house—my establishment consists of three female servants, my wife, and a male servant—they had all retired—I did not go into every room in the house at two o'clock—I was in the room, the window of which was open, when I went to bed—it was the last room I visited—I am positive I closed the window myself—there is no fastening to it—I examined the marks on the balcony, within about an hour after the prisoners were taken—I am quite sure I kept hold of the man in the passage, and when the light came, I found it was Huggett—I only struggled with one man—the house is in the parish of St. Mary, Lambeth—it is all one parish, Lambeth.
Cross-examined by MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. Where is the coal cellar? A. Under the pavement in Bridge-street—there is an area between the house and pavement, and an iron over it—you can get to the coal cellar from the kitchen, or round from the kitchen stairs into the passage leading to the coal cellar—there are several other cellars in that passage—there is one at the bottom of the kitchen stairs—it was not quite ten minutes after I was alarmed that I found Jones in the coal cellar—a policeman went into the cellar and found
him—mine is a public-house—I have a pretty good custom—I generally keep open till about half-past twelve or one o'clock—the house is closed about half-past twelve o'clock.
MR. CROUCH. Q. Your house is near Astley's theatre? A. Yes.
COURT. Q. Could you tell from the sound whether there was more than one person? A. I could not tell—I heard no other sound—there were two decanters at the bottom of the stairs—I broke one of them in coming down—I consider the persons entered by the window.
HENRY PALMER ( police-constable B 53.) On Monday morning, the 14th of August, I was on duty on Westminster-bridge, and about half-past three o'clock I heard a noise inside this house—I called to another constable, suspecting there was something wrong—when we got to the house I saw a window open in the front, facing the water, near the balcony—I heard a noise inside, and called to Lutchford, the other constable, and told him my suspicion—he went round to the back part, down Stangate-street to the stable—Carpenter, another policeman, came up to me—Lutchford came back to me and returned to the back part of the house—No. 108 rang the bell in front of the house—Mr. Mackintosh looked out—we told him our suspicions—he said he would come down—in the mean time we heard a rush from the passage in the house leading to the back door—in a very short time I heard a great noise at the back part—I ran as bard as I could—in going I met a civilian —he told me to make haste, and when I got round to the yard by the back door I saw Lutchford struggling violently with the prisoner Trenate—I went close up to him, and saw Lutchford's face was covered with blood—he said to me, "Go on, there is more inside"—I immediately ran to the back door, and the civilian had procured a light—we went in together into the passage, and saw Mr. Mackintosh struggling violently with Huggett—I secured him—I saw no more till Jones was brought to the station—Trenate and Huggett were taken to the station together—they behaved very violently going along—I searched Huggett, but found nothing particular on his person; only some halfpence—I taw three halfpence drop from him going along.
HENRY LUTCHFORD ( police-constable L 87.) On the morning of the 14th of August I was on duty on Westminster-bridge, and saw 153 B—I went to the back of Mr. Mackintosh's premises—I had seen the window open before I went round—I went to the stable-yard and tried the back door—it was fastened—I returned to the front of the house and told the constable it was quite safe—I returned, in consequence of what he said, to the back of the premises immediately—the gates were not shut—as soon as I got up the stable-yard I heard a noise like bolts of the door being unfastened—it was the back door, which I had previously found fast—I saw it open, and saw two men make their appearance at the door—I could not discern their countenances at the time—it was just getting day-light—as soon as they saw me they retreated into the passage—I walked and stood a yard or so from the door—one of them said, "Stand off, you b—, or I will rip your b—guts out"—I was then within about a yard or a yard and a half of the back door—I told him it was no use, and bid them surrender, they should not pass that way—I was in my police dress—one of them said, "Let us go in and give it the b—" that was not the same voice—one of them then sprang towards me, and I knocked him down with my truncheon—I caught him somewhere by the side of the neck—that was Trenate—I was immediately knocked down by the second party, and received a violent blow over the eye with a life-preserver—I saw it, it was light enough in the yard to see at that time—I fell on Trenate, who I had knocked down, caught him by the collar, and kept hold of him with
my right hand—we were both on the ground together—the other made an attempt to leap over to make his escape, but in the act of doing so I caught him by the leg with my left hand, threw him over, and also secured him by the collar with my left handthat was Huggett—we all three got on our legs again together, and had a violent struggle together—Huggett struck me a second time on the left side of my hat with the life-preserver, which knocked my hat off—Trenate directly said, "Loose me, or I will job your b—eye out"—I had him in my right hand at the time by the collar—he immediately thrust the knife into the side of my eye—from what I could feel of it the knife went apparently under my eye—he also cut my nose afterwards—I was compelled to loose Huggett at that time—I caught Trenate's hand afterwards, but being so weak through loss of blood I could scarcely keep the knife off—he endeavoured to cut me again, and scratched my nose in several places—I saw the knife in his hand, and saw the blade—a constable knocked the knife out of his hand—assistance came up afterwards—53 B came into the yard—Huggett, after making his escape from me, retreated into the house again—Palmer came to me—I told him to go into the house, for there were more in the house—I took Trenate out into Stangate, where I was met I believe by a constable and a brewer's drayman, but I then became insensible—I lost a great deal of blood—I did not loose hold of Trenate at all, during the struggle—I am sure he is the person.
WILLIAM COCKRILL ( police-constable L 108.) On the 14th of Aug., about half-past three o'clock in the morning, my attention was called to the prosecutor's house, by seeing Palmer and Lutchford talking together at the foot of Westminster-bridge—when I got up to the corner of Stangate, Lutchford came towards me, and said he thought there was something wrong at Mr. Mackintosh's, we had better go round to the back, and see if it was safe, which he did—I went up to Palmer—he said something to me—I looked to see if there was any light through the doorway—it was all in darkness—I then went to the side door and heard some whispering and footsteps more from the direction of the bar towards the side entrance, leading to the landing of the stair-caseat that moment Lutchford returned and stated something—I sent him back again—after a short lapse of time I called out to Mr. Mackintosh several times, and as I rang the bell Mr. Mackintosh opened his bed-room window—I told him I thought thieves were in the house—he said, "I thought I heard them a quarter of an hour ago"—the next moment I heard the swing door which leads to the back entrance of the premises pulled back—it works on a pulley, and when the door closes again the pulley makes a noise, which would let me know that the door was closed—about a minute afterwards one of the witnesses came up to the corner of Stangate, and called out to me to send further assistance—I then sent Palmer round to the back of the premises—shortly afterwards I saw Lutchford in the road—he called out to me for assistance, in consequenoe of being stabbed by a knife—I ran to his assistance, and saw Trenate in the act of standing over him, and Lutchford hanging on to the waistband of his trowsers—Trenate had a knife in his hand—Lutchford was on the ground—he turned over twice while I was running from the bridge to where he was lying—I hit Trenate a blow with my fist on his head, and the knife fell down on the left, and he fell to the right of Lutchford—I secured the knife and the prisoner the next moment, before he got up from the ground—at the same moment there was a cry for further assistance at Mr. Mackintosh's—a drayman came up, and being a man I knew well, I asked him to take charge of Trenate, which he did, after I disarmed him—I then returned to the house, knocked at the door, and was admitted—when I got in I was told something by Banister—I
heard that three persons had been seen in the yard—I searched the house, and in the coal-cellar found Jones—this jemmy was found half or three quarters of an hour afterwards—this hat was on his head, and in it was a five-shilling piece and some false curls—I found eleven lucifers on his person at the station—this house is in the parish of Lambeth.
Cross-examined by MR. HUDDLESTONE. Q. St. Mary, Lambeth, is it? A. It is the parish of Lambeth—I do not know whether the parish church is St. Mary—I know it is the parish of Lambeth—this is the knife.
MR. CROUCH. Q. Was it covered with blood? A. Yes, and Trenate's hands also—there are the remains of blood, exactly as I found it—after I apprehended Jones my hat fell off, in consequence of a violent struggle which ensued—he threw me, and the knife fell out—in going down the road I saw Mr. Hinton, a saddler, and I gave the knife to him, as Jones was very violent.
MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. Whereabouts was Jones in the coal-cellar? A. At the far side, as far as he could get—he was on his face—he had no appearance of being in liquor—I did not smell his breath.
WILLIAM SANDFORD . I am foreman to Mr. Beard, and live in Red Lion-yard, at the foot of Westminster-bridge—it is the yard behind the prosecutor's premises—the back gates were open on the night in question, as my master was out—my attention was attracted by the policeman running about—he told me to stand back, which I did, till he had taken two of the prisoners—he called for a light, and I brought one—I picked up in the yard three spoons, a constable's staff, and some cigars—I gave them to the sergeant No. 3.
WILLIAM SIMONS ( police-sergeant L 3.) I received some articles from Sandford, which I produce—I found some cigars, and 1 found a jemmy in the coal-cellar—I dug it out of the coals—I found two german silver forks and a spoon in the bar-parlour, and a pair of plated sugar-tongs—the life-preserver was found on the cellar steps—I tried the jemmy to the bar window, and it fitted it.
Cross-examined by MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. Where in the cellar did you find the crow-bar? A. Under the coals, nearly in the centre of the cellar—I found it about twenty minutes after I first came up—I did not go into the house the first time—I went through the kitchen, down the stairs, into the coal-cellar—there might be, perhaps, a ton of coals in the cellar.
MATTHEW FRENCH WAGSTAFF . I am surgeon to the police-force—I saw Mr. Mackintosh at the station on the morning, I was sent for—he appeared to have a contused wound on the temple—it was done by some heavy instrument—this life-preserver would have done it—there is danger attending a blow on that part—the eye has been discoloured and swollen—it is very slightly so now.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Was it not such a blow as might have been given by a man's fist? A. I think not—it must have been some heavy instrument—the skin was not broken, but there was that appearance to lead me to suppose it was some heavy instrument—it is possible a violent blow with a fist might do it.
MR. MACKINTOSH re-examined. Q. What was the length of time between your receiving the blow and what took place outside in the yard? A. I believe that must have taken place at the same time—I heard no sound of violence outside—I never heard more than one foot in the house.
TRENATE — GUILTY . Aged 21.— HUGGETT — GUILTY . Aged 23.—
JONES — GUILTY . Aged 23.— Death Recorded.
(There was another indictment against Trenate and Huggett for wounding the policeman.)
Before Mr. Baron Rolfe.

2574. THOMAS CONNOLLY was indicted for stealing, on the 8th of August, 1 counterpane, value 5 s.; the goods of Robert Garnham.
SARAH PRIOR . I am barmaid at the Nine Elms public-house, Battersea, kept by Mr. Robert Garnham. On the 8th of August, between one and two o'clock in the day, I saw the prisoner in the yard with the counterpane in a basket—he went out into the road—I gave information, and saw him throw the basket over some palings—I am sure he is the boy—I saw him stopped.
SUSAN GARNHAM . I am the wife of Robert Garnham. I saw the prisoner going off with the basket, and throw it over the fence into a garden—this counterpane, which is mine, was in it—it had been hanging out in the tea-gardens.
HENRY BROOKSON ( police-constable V 60.) I received charge of the prisoner.
Prisoner's Defence. I was by the side of the railway, and I saw the train coming in—I ran to get a job, and was stopped.
GUILTY .* Aged 18.— Confined Six Months.

2577. GEORGE TWINE was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of August, 1 handkerchief, value 9 d.; the goods of William Ruffell Stevenson.
WILLIAM RUFFELL STEVENSON . I am in the employ of Mr. Russell, a job-master, of Plough-yard, Clapham. On Friday night, the 11th of August, I placed two handkerchiefs and something else on the footboard of a fly in a coach-house in my master's yard—there are large gates, and room for seven or eight carriages to stand—I went to work at half-past six o'clock next morning, and found an old handkerchief which I had left, but I missed the one now produced—I saw it again in possession of the policeman—it is mine—there is about an inch in one corner that has been hemmed, but it is picked out now—I can see the place where it has been hemmed—if it is held up to the light you will see the holes—I will swear to it.
STEPHEN FARMER ( police-constable V 154.) On the 12th of August the prisoner passed me in Clapham, about twenty minutes before two o'clock in the morning—I followed him for about 250 yards—when he got to the turning to the back of Mr. Russell's stable I lost sight of him—I afterwards went round and searched, but could not find him—I found the front gates open, which five minutes before I met him were fast.
SAMUEL EASTMAN ( police-constable V 132.) I received information from Farmer, and apprehended the prisoner—he had a handkerchief with him like this one, but I could not swear it was this.
WILLIAM EDMAN ( police-constable V 179.) I received information, went to the station, and took this handkerchief from the prisoner's hat—he said he bought it in the New-cut—one corner of it has been hemmed for about an inch, but has been now unpicked.
Prisoner's Defence. I had the handkerchief in my hat when I was taken to the station, and I took it to wash myself with before all the policemen, which I should not have done if I had stolen it.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

2578. WILLIAM HANDCOCK was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of June, 20 paving-stones, value 2 l. 10 s., the goods of Joseph Sargent and another, his masters.
JOSEPH SARGENT . I am in partnership with Charles Denton. We employed the prisoner—I did not authorise him to sell any paving-stones—I have seen some stones since—they correspond with those we had at Paradise-street, but I could not swear to them—I never authorised the prisoner to part with them.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. You were directed to take up these stones? A. Yes; we had a contract with the parish of Rotherhithe to take them up, and lay down new ones.
COURT. Q. Were they under your care and custody? A. Yes; we were answerable to carry them to a laystall belonging to the commissioners—they had been in our carts—I had not the care, the custody, or the possession of them.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18430821-2579]
2579. WILLIAM HANDCOCK was again indicted for stealing, on the 19th of June, 30 paving-stones, value 1 l. 13 s., the goods of Joseph Sargent and another, his masters.
JOSEPH SARGENT . I am in partnership with Charles Denton. We undertook to take up the old paving at Rotherhithe, and lay down new—we took up the old ones—we employed the prisoner, but he was not authorised or allowed to sell them—I have seen the stones produced by Mr. Heath and Mr. Miller—they are similar to what were at Rotherhithe.
WILLIAM HEATH . I bought about twenty pieces of stones of Mr. Winter, Mr. Sargent has seen them, and I think they are the stones he has identified.
ROBERT WINTER . The stones I sold to Mr. Heath I bought of the prisoner—I found them at Rotherhithe—I paid him 2 d. a foot for them—they amounted to 1 l. 2 s. 6 d.
Prisoner. I had the stuff given me. Witness. I believe Mr. Nottingham, the commissioner, gave the prisoner some stones; I was present, and believe I heard him give them to him—I told the Magistrate so—Mr. Nottingham, is since dead.
NOT GUILTY .
